♻ ☆ Periodic and Harmonic Periodic RoPE for Length Extrapolation in LLMs
Simin Huo, Ning Li
The ability to process long contexts is crucial for large language models (LLMs) to perform long-horizon tasks. Rotary position embedding (RoPE) gives every token a new rotation angle, so a model eventually meets angles it never saw in training, i.e., position exhaustion. We propose Periodic RoPE (P-RoPE), a positional encoding mechanism that operates in conjunction with sliding window attention. It repeats the first sliding-window-size \(W\) angles of the RoPE table, so the model needs only \(W\) distinct angles. P-RoPE works but introduces a wrap error at the window boundary. We further propose Harmonic Periodic RoPE (HP-RoPE), which removes the error by choosing frequencies that complete whole turns in \(W\) tokens. We compare models that share the same architecture, data, and schedule and differ only in the window-layer position encoding: standard RoPE, P-RoPE, and HP-RoPE. The results show that P-RoPE and HP-RoPE keep perplexity nearly flat from the training length \(768\) to \(16\)k, while RoPE, NTK, and YaRN rise. In a format-free Passkey test at \(16\)k, they remain at \(99\)--\(100\%\), against \(11\)--\(37\%\) for those RoPE interpolations. The code is available at \href{https://github.com/Cominder/miniwin}{https://github.com/Cominder/miniwin}.

♻ ☆ Multi-Objective Structured Pruning of LLMs for Latency and Model Size Optimization
Muhammad Junaid Ali, Smail Niar, El-Ghazali Talbi
Large Language Models (LLMs) have achieved widespread adoption because of their strong reasoning and query-response capabilities. However, deploying them in embedded and edge computing environments remains challenging because of strict latency, memory, and energy constraints. Their large parameter counts and computational demands hinder efficient execution on resource-constrained platforms. Although model pruning has emerged as a viable solution for reducing scale while preserving performance, jointly optimizing layers, attention heads, and Multi-Layer Perceptron (MLP) dimensions remains highly complex. Exhaustively exploring this combined design space is computationally expensive and often leads to local optima or unstable configurations. To address these limitations, we propose a hardware-aware, multi-objective structured pruning framework. The proposed two-stage method explicitly targets latency and model size for efficient deployment on edge devices. In the coarse-grained stage, multi-objective depth pruning removes entire attention and MLP blocks to reduce computational load and memory usage. In the subsequent fine-grained stage, Parallel Bayesian Optimization (PBO) searches for the optimal layer-wise pruning ratios for pruning under latency constraints, while importance-based strategies rank the specific components to be pruned within each layer's allocated budget. Experimental results show that our approach reduces model complexity with minimal impact on commonsense reasoning tasks and zero-shot performance. Our method achieves a favorable trade-off among accuracy, latency, and model size, making it suitable for edge deployment. Across multiple LLMs at 37.5% and 50% pruning ratios, the proposed approach achieves better performance on commonsense reasoning tasks than existing methods while significantly reducing inference cost.
♻ ☆ Can Computation from Earlier Problems Help LLMs Solve New Ones?
Jipei He, Wenhui Tan, Xiaoyi Yu, Enver Sangineto, Fiorenzo Parascandolo, Rita Cucchiara, Ruihua Song
Large language models often solve independent problems in the same conversation. Can computation from earlier problems help them solve new ones? Preliminary experiments show that retained history can raise or lower later-turn accuracy, even within the same domain. To trace this effect, we hold each problem fixed and vary its preceding history. The resulting changes in the model's internal states contain a recurring, problem-dependent component. To improve reasoning with retained history, we introduce STAIR (Stale-Token Attention for Inter-query Reuse). It captures attention keys and values from earlier response generation in a read-only bank. STAIR learns to reflect current query vectors to redirect attention over this bank during prompt processing. The backbone and stored entries remain fixed; only 12,288 parameters are trained. Across three Qwen models and four reasoning benchmarks, STAIR improves average later-turn accuracy by up to 11.67 percentage points over the unmodified model with history. The math-trained Qwen3.5-9B controller also improves code generation on HumanEval+ without further training.

♻ ☆ TELEVAL: A Benchmark Designed for Spoken Language Models in Chinese Interactive Scenarios
Zehan Li, Hongjie Chen, Qing Wang, Yuxin Zhang, Jing Zhou, Hang Lv, Mengjie Du, Yaodong Song, Jie Lian, Jian Kang, Jie Li, Yongxiang Li
Spoken Language Models (SLMs) are expected to support natural spoken interaction beyond task completion. Existing benchmarks assess a range of semantic tasks and conversational capabilities. However, high task scores do not necessarily imply reliable and appropriate spoken interaction. To address this gap, we introduce TELEVAL, a large-scale benchmark focused on Chinese spoken interaction. It evaluates responses to spoken inputs without additional task-specific prompts or predefined answer options. TELEVAL covers two complementary aspects: (1) Reliable Content Fulfillment, which measures semantic accuracy under diverse acoustic and linguistic conditions, and (2) Interactional Appropriateness, which assesses conversational naturalness and appropriate adaptation to auditory cues. Experiments reveal limitations in both aspects. Strong results on factual question-answering datasets do not consistently extend to locally relevant knowledge or challenging speech conditions, such as Chinese cultural questions and dialectal speech. Models also struggle to adapt their responses to auditory cues, even in cases where they explicitly recognize them. We further document a recurring failure pattern, termed the "Caption Trap", in which some responses describe a perceived auditory cue instead of addressing the user's conversational needs. Overall, the evaluated SLMs struggle to deliver consistently accurate and conversationally appropriate responses. TELEVAL provides a diagnostic framework for evaluating both content reliability and conversational adaptation.
♻ ☆ Does Merge Form a Hopf Algebra? Coproducts, Traces, and the Prospects of Algebraic Minimalism
Daisuke Bekki
Marcolli, Chomsky, and Berwick (2025; henceforth MCB) formulate the Merge operation of Minimalism on a Hopf algebra of Connes-Kreimer type, and claim that language and physics obey the same algebraic structure. This paper examines the premise of that claim: whether Merge on workspaces can be described as a Hopf algebra. We first survey five coproducts for coassociativity and a connected grading: the Connes-Kreimer coproduct $Δ_ρ$, MCB's two coproducts $Δ_d$ and $Δ_c$, the contraction coproduct of Marcolli and Skigin (2025), and the coproduct of Xiao (2026). $Δ_d$ is not coassociative. For $Δ_c$, MCB gives no rule for the labels of trace leaves in trees that already contain trace leaves; under the literal extension it is not coassociative, but once a rule and a grading are supplied it yields a graded connected Hopf algebra. The coproduct of Xiao is coassociative, and so is that of Marcolli and Skigin once its definition is extended to trees with trace leaves. These properties do not tell the coassociative coproducts apart; what distinguishes them is the representation that movement leaves behind. We then review how generative grammar has treated moved elements, and confirm that semantic interpretation requires two things of the representation that results from movement. Retention requires that the structure the lower position had before movement be recoverable; Identifiability requires that, in interpreting the lower position, it be determinable which upper element, if any, corresponds to it. None of the five coproducts meets both conditions: Retention is met only by $Δ_c$, and there only in the form of a label, and Identifiability by none. In light of the results on coassociativity, we also reassess the Birkhoff factorization that MCB employs at the syntax-semantics interface. Finally, we indicate at least three directions for algebraic Minimalism.
comment: 26 pages. v2: substantially revised. Corrects v1's claim that the contraction coproduct cannot be coassociative while retaining the labels of traces; adds the coproducts of Marcolli and Skigin (2025) and Xiao (2026); restates the requirement of semantic interpretation as two conditions on the representation that results from movement (Retention and Identifiability), in light of Collins (2026)
♻ ☆ Gradients for Interventions and Activation Values for Detection: Targeted Feature Learning in Language Models
Jonathan Drechsel, Steffen Herbold
Model-internal features can be studied through both their ability to identify a specified concept and their causal effect when manipulated, e.g., through steering or weight editing. A prominent approach to feature learning is Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs), which learn broad feature dictionaries whose relation to particular concepts is typically identified post hoc. However, many interpretability questions are instead hypothesis-driven and concern a concept specified in advance. We study this setting as targeted feature learning, where a single feature is constructed for such a predefined concept. We present a controlled comparison across three model signals (activation values, activation gradients, and parameter gradients) and two estimators (contrastive mean and a learned one-dimensional encoder-decoder), yielding six targeted methods, with CAA and GRADIEND as existing instances and four new methods covering the remaining combinations. We compare these methods against pretrained SAEs across 15 tasks and ten language models, evaluating both detection and causal intervention. Across models, the strongest detection performance is achieved by contrastive activation value methods, whereas the strongest intervention performance is achieved by gradient signals, most strongly with a contrastive mean. Notably, within the contrastive mean family, activation gradients substantially improve intervention over activation values without requiring parameter space interventions. Overall, our results show that targeted feature quality depends jointly on the model signal and estimator, with detection and intervention capturing complementary properties.
♻ ☆ Wieszcz-XIX: A 3.1-Billion-Word Corpus of Pre-1918 Polish and Temporally Bounded Language Models Trained From Scratch
Szymon Kocur
Historical Polish is well documented as a language but annotated in machine-readable form only to about a million words for the period this paper covers; the rest sits behind optical character recognition of variable quality. We present Wieszcz-XIX, a corpus of 6.75 billion tokens (about 3.1 billion words) in 294,369 documents, most of them periodical issues, of Polish published from 1800 to 1918, assembled from Wolne Lektury and the Internet Archive by a pipeline that filters, deduplicates, audits for post-1918 leakage and splits at the document level. It is over three orders of magnitude larger than the annotated corpus of the same period, and we quantify its defects: recognition corruption against a false-positive floor, near-identical duplication, which is removed, and post-1918 leakage, which is excluded from the training corpus itself down to a known residue of 0.04 to 0.38% of its bytes, found in the transcribed source, so the published corpus is the trained one document for document. On a hand-corrected sample the character error rate is 0.68% where the text is legible, and 45% of the sampled passages cannot be corrected. On it we train a ladder of decoder-only models from 47M to 349M parameters from scratch, and measure their temporal boundedness. Against two modern Polish base models, one far larger, the 349M shows a crossover, as does the 107M against the comparator of its size: post-1918 vocabulary costs them about 3.1 bits per byte more than period vocabulary, a gap the comparators do not show, and period vocabulary costs them fewer bits than it costs the comparators. Shown period text, the models keep its spelling and the comparators only partly. Adding parameters gains about twice as much as a second pass over the data. We release the corpus, code and weights. Content warning: the models reproduce period prejudice, including antisemitic statements.

♻ ☆ SynthDCB: World Distillation for Scalable Device Control Benchmarking
Zichen Zhu, Dingye Liu, Nan Jiang, Danyang Zhang, Hang Zheng, Shijie Zheng, Situo Zhang, Kunyao Lan, Yingzi Ma, Sheng Jiang, Lu Chen, Kai Yu
Scaling device-control evaluation requires scaling both interactive worlds and the tasks agents must solve. We introduce SynthDCB, a benchmark for environment awareness and device control built through world distillation (WD). World distillation transforms knowledge of device behaviors, constraints, and environmental effects into modular executable components. A unified capability representation connects tool discovery, world execution, and task synthesis. SynthDCB instantiates Home, Car, and Phone with 114 device classes, 1,166 functions, and 95 events, supporting five task families with 32 subtypes. Explicit states and tool-call traces support task validation and agent evaluation. We evaluate 22 models on 2,560 test tasks; the best achieves 88.8% mean Pass@1. Construction experiments demonstrate token efficiency and expansion of task coverage. At the richest knowledge level, WD fully covers 22/23 properties, 19/29 functionalities, and 30/39 event requirements specified in a real RobotVacuum protocol. Furthermore, the 1,000 sampled tasks semantically cover 81.6% of the 854 reference instructions in the real-world corpus. SynthDCB connects an expanding space of real-to-sim executable capabilities to verifiable agent evaluation.

♻ ☆ Formalizing Formal Semantics: The Formal Atlas
Stergios Chatzikyriakidis, Aarne Talman
In this paper we present the Formal Atlas, a system for the study of natural language semantics using proof assistants. Our aim is to formalize semantic accounts, check their predictions and compare the implemented theories. To this end, we connect source records and formal developments with an interactive application, where researchers can inspect proofs and modify supported Rocq files. Montague's PTQ and Gallin provide our main cases, followed by dynamic and type-theoretic comparisons, RSA and distributional semantics. We also discuss what it takes to formalize less explicit analyses. Lastly, we consider the use of AI, both for assistance in developing these resources and for inference systems that can make use of them. This work extends an earlier programme in proof-assistant semantics, with research and teaching as complementary uses.
♻ ☆ Polar: A Benchmark for Evaluating Political Bias in LLMs AACL
Sangho Kim, Heejin Kim, Hyunggeun Jeon, Yoonhee Park, Jaejin Lee
Political bias in large language models (LLMs) is increasingly significant, but difficult to measure reproducibly across political and linguistic contexts. We introduce Polar, a 4,026-instance multiple-choice benchmark that measures political bias through option-level likelihoods rather than prompt-based generation. Polar covers two ideological axes and eight issue categories derived from the Manifesto Project, and evaluates models in parallel across U.S. and South Korean political contexts. Across 38 LLMs, measured bias varies systematically with political context, issue category, model group, and presentation language. All models lean left-progressive on U.S. political content, but show more centered and mixed patterns on South Korean content. Translation experiments further show that presentation language alone can shift measured bias. These findings highlight the need for multilingual and cross-contextual evaluation of political bias in LLMs. The code and dataset are publicly available on GitHub (https://github.com/mcrl/Polar) and Hugging Face (https://huggingface.co/datasets/thunder-research-group/Polar).

♻ ☆ Persistent Sparse Autoencoders: Learning Feature-Specific Timescales in Language Model Representations
Haoyan Luo, Mateo Espinosa Zarlenga, Mateja Jamnik
Sparse autoencoders (SAEs) decompose language model activations into sparse features, yet these models traditionally encode each token independently, failing to expose information that persists across a sequence. We first show that temporal persistence can naturally emerge in standard SAE features: after a feature activates, the hidden state remains aligned with its direction, and past activations reduce the reconstruction error of later hidden states. How long this lasts varies widely across features. We therefore introduce Persistent Sparse Autoencoders (Persistent SAEs), an extension of standard SAEs that learns a persistence coefficient for each feature, allowing feature-specific timescales to emerge from reconstruction. Our experiments show that Persistent SAEs retain competitive reconstruction quality while learning a spectrum of timescales: short-timescale (fast) features remain locally interpretable, whereas long-timescale (slow) features accumulate higher-level contextual information. Moreover, we show in a prompt-injection monitoring case study that slow features retain injection signals over long contexts and causally affect monitor judgements. These results suggest that Persistent SAEs offer new opportunities for interpreting and monitoring language models via persistent features.
♻ ☆ DEO: Training-Free Direct Embedding Optimization for Negation-Aware Retrieval ACL 2026
Taegyeong Lee, Jiwon Park, Seunghyun Hwang, JooYoung Jang
Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) and Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) have enabled diverse retrieval methods. However, existing retrieval methods often fail to accurately retrieve results for negation and exclusion queries. To address this limitation, prior approaches rely on embedding adaptation or fine-tuning, which introduce additional computational cost and deployment complexity. We propose Direct Embedding Optimization (DEO), a training-free method for negation-aware text and multimodal retrieval. DEO decomposes queries into positive and negative components and optimizes the query embedding with a contrastive objective. Without additional training data or model updates, DEO outperforms baselines on NegConstraint, with gains of +0.0738 nDCG@10 and +0.1028 MAP@100, while improving Recall@5 by +6\% over OpenAI CLIP in multimodal retrieval. These results demonstrate the practicality of DEO for negation- and exclusion-aware retrieval in real-world settings.

♻ ☆ EDITORS Know Your Style! Editing LoRA Subspaces for Stylistic Attribution and Imitation NeurIPS 2026
Zixuan Wang, Gregory Kang Ruey Lau, Xiao Tian, Jue Fan, Caroline Chaux, Bryan Kian Hsiang Low
Disentangling writing style from semantic content is a fundamental challenge in literary text modeling. Style-content entanglement causes models to rely on semantic content for authorship attribution (AA) tasks and memorize author-specific content for style imitation (SI) tasks. We propose EDITORS (EditingLoRA Subspaces), a diagnose-then-deflate framework that adapts pretrained large language models and prioritizes stylistic features, making it effective for both AA and SI tasks. EDITORS first trains a diagnostic LoRA adapter using style-neutral statements derived from the training corpus to identify a content-oriented subspace. It then trains the final style adapter under activation-space deflation that projects input activations away from the identified content-oriented directions. We empirically demonstrate that EDITORS is effective. On three AA benchmarks spanning literature, social media, and news, EDITORS achieves state-of-the-art accuracy, outperforming the strongest existing baseline by up to 29%. On SI, EDITORS generates stylistically faithful and diverse passages, outperforming baselines in achieving style similarity while adhering to content specifications.

♻ ☆ MedPsy: State-of-the-Art Small Medical Language Models for Efficient Edge Deployment NeurIPS 2026
Davide Vitabile, Alexandro Buffa, Akshay Nambiar, Amril Nazir
Medical language models promise expert clinical support, but the strongest open-source LLMs remain too large for private edge-device deployment, while compact medical models still trail substantially on knowledge-intensive and real-world healthcare tasks. Reasoning models that fit edge memory budgets can produce long reasoning traces, increasing sequential decoding, end-to-end latency, energy consumption, and KV-cache memory pressure. We present MedPsy, a family of text-only 1.7B and 4B medical language models designed for edge deployment, targeting this accuracy-generation-efficiency trade-off. Our recipe combines a synthetic medical data pipeline over biology, medicine, and health seeds with chain-of-thought targets from a 235B medical-focused LLM teacher; a four-stage post-training curriculum comprising two SFT stages followed by two RL stages with hard-sample mining; and an edge deployment evaluation spanning multiple GGUF quantization variants and real-device inference on laptop and smartphone hardware. On seven closed-ended medical benchmarks, MedPsy-4B surpasses MedGemma-1.5-4B by 19.34 points and matches MedGemma-27B while being 6.75x smaller; MedPsy-1.7B outperforms MedGemma-1.5-4B by 11.42 points despite being less than half its size. On HealthBench-Hard, MedPsy-4B surpasses MedGemma-27B by 15.33 points, while MedPsy-1.7B surpasses it by 11.66 points despite being ~16x smaller. Beyond accuracy, MedPsy reduces average response length by 1.7x (1.7B) and 3.2x (4B) versus its Qwen3 backbones, while Q4_K_M quantization preserves the aggregate score within 1 point of BF16 with ~69% lower disk footprint. In real-device inference, MedPsy-4B's shorter generations translate into 75% lower median end-to-end latency and estimated energy per answer than its Qwen3 backbone at matched quantization, while peak RAM remains below 5 GB across our tested laptop and smartphone configurations.

♻ ☆ Characterize Then Distill: Mechanistic Reasoning in Large Output Spaces
Debjyoti Saha Roy
Reasoning-trained language models can pick a document's few labels from thousands of candidates, zero-shot. We ask which components make these choices, and whether a student can be taught where to look as well as what to write, so its decisions trace to a few components and what they read. We study clinical coding of MIMIC-IV discharge summaries with all 5,651 candidate codes in the prompt. Each choice is one token: a region of the code list, a code within it, or the departure from a similar code named earlier. We measure each attention head's causal effect there by removing it and transplanting it into another summary. On essentially every summary, a small, shared set of heads, partly specific to each kind of choice, is necessary and sufficient; blocking what the tested heads read removes their effect. MISTILL builds on this: besides the teacher's text, it supervises where the student attends at the teacher's decision tokens. In a cross-family student, its own heads, found after training, restore nearly twice as much of the choice between similar codes as before, where chain-of-thought distillation alone adds under a third; one that already carried most of it gains a little, consistently across seeds, and both gains hold on the students' own completed traces. On two corpora with coder-marked evidence, a MISTILL student's decision heads attend to it more, and more specifically, than a chain-of-thought-distilled student's. Controls locate the gain in supervising attention at the decision tokens; in the cross-family student, a target built from unselected teacher heads loses it. No task difference from chain-of-thought distillation is detected at matched precision. The structure recurs in a second reasoner; ranked heads beat random ones on legal-document labelling and code generation; and without retraining the students keep their gain on Spanish case reports and a QA benchmark.

♻ ☆ Swiss-Knife: A Framework for Reconfigurable Externalised Multi-Objective Alignment at Decode Time
Agnibh Karmakar, Mayur Parvatikar, Shreyash Dhoot, Amit Dhanda, Aman Chadha, Kapil Wanaskar, Vinija Jain, Amitava Das
Decode-time alignment methods steer a frozen language model by scoring candidate continuations with an external reward and selecting the maximiser. We argue that this shared design is a single degenerate point in a much larger space. We introduce Swiss-Knife, a framework for externalised multi-objective alignment in which the alignment specification is a first-class runtime object: hot-swappable scoring blades, a batch normaliser, a pairwise aggregation operator, and a selection rule. Six axioms characterise the admissible aggregation operators, and we prove a representation theorem: every operator satisfying them has the form $R_i = \sum_{j \neq i} g((μ_i - μ_j)/s(σ_i,σ_j))$, a two-parameter family containing probit and logistic comparison rules and pointwise argmax as named coordinates. Within it, pairwise aggregation is Lipschitz-stable under adversarial reward contamination while argmax is not, and Candidate-Batch Normalization (CBN) makes the weight simplex invariant to the rescalings under which reward models are only ever identified. Our reference instantiation pairs DPO-LoRA blades with an uncertainty-aware pairwise tournament. Sweeping the helpfulness/honesty/harmlessness simplex, it attains the best balanced frontier of six decode-time methods (harmonic $F_1$ 0.797 vs. 0.750 for the strongest baseline, $p < 10^{-14}$) with the lowest refusal rate and highest helpfulness of any arm, and reconfigures its objectives in 0.05 ms with no gradient computation.

♻ ☆ Self-Rewarded Reinforcement Learning for Better-Structured Medical Explanations in Small Language Models
Prakhar Mishra, Bhavya Yajush Awasthi, Tanuja, Nishant
Small language models (SLMs) can run on commodity hardware and support privacy-preserving, on-premises clinical deployment, but their medical explanations are often short and poorly structured compared with large language models. Recent supervised approaches such as Meerkat transfer chain-of-thought reasoning into 7B-8B backbones, yet their training objective does not directly target the style or organization of the generated rationales. Building on the instruction-tuned Mistral-7B backbone, we investigate whether a lightweight reinforcement learning (RL) step can improve explanation structure without human preference labels. We apply Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) with LoRA adaptation to 2000 Meerkat-derived medical prompts, guided by a simple self-supervised reward that favors longer, lexically diverse, and well-punctuated responses while penalizing very short ones. Training for about 170 PPO steps on a single NVIDIA A100 GPU yields a stable increase in the average reward. Quantitative and qualitative comparisons show that the RL-tuned model produces longer, more organized, multi-point explanations while preserving the underlying medical content. We discuss the promise and the limitations of self-rewarded RL for shaping the style of medical explanations.

♻ ☆ Geometry-bridge flow matching for blind rigid-motion correction in head cone-beam CT
Sungho Yun, Seungryong Cho
Rigid motion of a patient head during head cone-beam CT (CBCT) scan can degrade image quality and complicate recovery of accurate anatomical structures from the measured projections. We present a 3D flow-matching prior approach that learns the image changes induced by motion. Its geometry-bridge connects motion-corrupted and motion-free Feldkamp-Davis-Kress (FDK) reconstructed images of the same source volume through progressively reduced simulated motion. The forward projector's geometry derivative supplies analytic velocity targets along this path. A plug-and-play loop encompasses the learned image updates together with spline-based pose estimation, conjugate-gradient data consistency, and image total-variation regularization. Both the refined image volume and the FDK reconstructed image volume at the estimated poses are evaluated. On simulated 360-view scans from 30 held-out CQ500 patients with nominal per-component motion amplitudes within 10 mm / 10 degrees, the framework achieved state-of-the-art image reconstruction quality and motion accuracy in comparison with other methos such as learned autofocus and diffusion-based joint reconstruction. Mean peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) reached 37.1 dB and mean detector-plane reprojection error (RPE) was 0.27 mm. With matched endpoints at the inference settings, the geometry bridge improved PSNR by 1.6 dB and reduced RPE by 33% relative to image-linear training. With 50 prior updates and coarse-to-fine motion estimation, total processing time averaged 9.1 minutes per patient on an RTX A6000.
♻ ☆ Supportive Token Revealing for Fast Diffusion Language Model Decoding
Giries Abu Ayoub, Mario Barbara, Lluís Pastor-Pérez, Tanja Bien, Aneesh Barthakur, Alaa Maalouf, Loay Mualem
Discrete diffusion language models can generate text efficiently by updating multiple masked positions in parallel, but this parallelism introduces a quality-latency trade-off. Aggressive decoding may commit mutually dependent tokens too early, while conservative decoding requires many denoising steps. Existing methods address this tension by deciding which tokens are safe to reveal using confidence or dependency criteria. However, avoiding unsafe commits does not necessarily make the remaining masked sequence easy to decode, since uncertain tokens may depend on masked tokens, creating a bottleneck for denoising steps. We propose AXON, a training-free module that can be added on top of existing parallel decoding strategies for diffusion language models. Rather than replacing the base decoder, AXON monitors the remaining uncertain masked tokens and intervenes only when their current state suggests that additional context is needed. It then shifts the criterion from which tokens are safest to reveal to which confident reveals would best support later denoising. AXON selects anchors, confident masked tokens that uncertain positions attend to, using attention, uncertainty, and confidence signals. Experiments on reasoning and code-generation benchmarks across multiple diffusion language models show that AXON improves the quality-latency trade-off of existing parallel decoders, often reducing the number of function evaluations while maintaining or improving accuracy.
♻ ☆ Clause-Factored Bilateral State Grounding and Scoped Passivity for Counterfactual Open-World Deductive Reasoning
Zeyu Jia, Bo Yao, Zihao Yang
Open-World Assumption (OWA) deduction requires distinguishing provable facts (True), refutations (False), and unprovable statements (Unknown) across counterfactual edits and unseen domains. Sequence and latent-recurrent transformers rely on distributional shortcuts, whereas chain-of-thought language models incur high autoregressive latency. We decompose OWA deduction into a clause-factored bilateral grounder ($F$) and a deductive deliberation operator ($B$), forming $B \circ F$ in Typed Edge-Factorized Bilateral Port-Hamiltonian Deliberation (TE-BPHD). Trained solely on three-way query labels without parsers or proof-depth annotations, TE-BPHD (37.85M parameters) maps clauses into a 1,440-dimensional Belnap space and propagates support through passivity-balanced implicit Port-Hamiltonian rule diodes. Across 17,036 locked ProofWriter test queries, TE-BPHD reaches 79.17 +/- 5.48% depth-5 counterfactual consistency (+23.83 to +38.67 percentage points over Seq2Seq T5, Flan-T5, and Recurrent-Depth T5) and 94.95 +/- 1.23% out-of-distribution (OOD) accuracy across 5,270 six-domain queries (92.54 +/- 3.38% macro-mean). On a paired 120-query OOD slice, TE-BPHD attains 93.61 +/- 4.59% versus 79.17% for 3-shot Qwen3-1.7B under a 2,048-token reasoning cap (+14.44 percentage points) at 9.44 versus 14,878.4 ms/query batch-16 NVIDIA L4 throughput. Operator interventions show that symmetric single-rule passivity prevents positive two-cycle modes seen in Gain-Matched Unilateral Horn (+50.00 percentage points counterfactual consistency), whereas unit-interval Grounded Product-T-Norm Logic Tensor Networks (LTN) achieve cycle-safe OOD transfer (94.50 +/- 0.87%) without linear solves, and multi-fact ParaRules prose exposes an upstream grounding trade-off (1.33 +/- 1.76% zero-shot vs. 34.11 +/- 5.01% with five-slot optimal-transport joint grounding).

♻ ☆ Think Multilingual, Not Harder: A Framework for Analyzing and Teaching Code-Switched Reasoning
Eleanor M. Lin, David Jurgens
Reasoning capabilities have enabled large language models to solve increasingly complex tasks. Interestingly, while reasoning models are often trained to generate monolingual text, these models have also been observed to code-switch (i.e., mix languages). Prior works have either viewed code-switching as an undesirable error, attempted to control code-switching through modifications to input prompts or the output decoding process, or focus on narrow subsets of languages, domains, tasks, and models. We address these gaps by introducing the first linguistically and behaviorally motivated fine-tuning framework for identifying beneficial code-switched reasoning behaviors in large language models and teaching these models to code-switch more effectively for reasoning. First, we create the Code-Switched Reasoning (CoRe) corpus, consisting of 7k reasoning traces from 15 models, 18 languages, 10 scripts, and diverse reasoning domains, providing insights into potentially helpful code-switching behaviors. Then, we expand CoRe into the Extended Code-Switched Reasoning corpus (EnCoRe) with 40 carefully curated datasets for training and evaluating six interventions on code-switching in reasoning across three models and seven languages, totaling 120 fine-tuning conditions. Across 80k+ reasoning traces from both language/culture-agnostic and -specific evaluations, English-dominated reasoning, semantically accurate code-switching, and a more even mix of languages are positively associated with correct answers, whereas dense switching is associated with incorrect answers. Moreover, we are able to elicit positive behaviors through fine-tuning tasks that do not directly demonstrate code-switching. Our work suggests that small but well-curated datasets can change how reasoning models code-switch, allowing us to reap the benefits of reasoning in the many languages that lack large-scale reasoning data.

♻ ☆ AVSD: Adaptive-View Self-Distillation by Balancing Consensus and Teacher-Specific Privileged Signals NeurIPS 2026
Duy Nguyen, Hanqi Xiao, Archiki Prasad, Zaid Khan, Anirban Das, Shi-Xiong Zhang, Sambit Sahu, Hyunji Lee, Elias Stengel-Eskin, Mohit Bansal
Self-distillation enables language models to learn on-policy from their own trajectories by using the same model as both student and teacher, with the teacher being conditioned on privileged information unavailable to the student. Such information can come in different types or views, such as solutions, demonstrations, feedback, or final answers. This setup provides dense token-level feedback without relying on a separate external model, but creates a fundamental asymmetry: the teacher may rely on view-specific information that the student cannot access at inference time. Moreover, the best type of privileged information is often task-dependent, making it difficult to choose a single teacher view. In this work, we address both these challenges jointly by introducing AVSD (Adaptive-View Self-Distillation), a novel method of self-distillation with multiple privileged-information views, which reconstructs token-level supervision by separating stable cross-view consensus from view-specific residual signals. AVSD identifies the consensus signal shared across views, which provides a reliable update direction, and then selectively adds the view-specific residual signal to adjust the update magnitude when it both aligns with the consensus direction and remains proportionate to the consensus signal. Experiments on math competition benchmarks (AIME24, AIME25, and HMMT25) show that AVSD consistently outperforms both single-view self-distillation baselines and GRPO, achieving average Avg@8 gains of 3.1% and 2.2% over the strongest baselines on Qwen3-8B and Qwen3-4B, respectively. Moreover, on code-generation benchmarks (Codeforces, LiveCodeBench v6) using Qwen3-8B, AVSD outperforms the single-view self-distillation baseline by 2.4% on average.

♻ ☆ Geometric Factual Recall in Transformers
Shauli Ravfogel, Gilad Yehudai, Joan Bruna, Alberto Bietti
How do transformer language models memorize factual associations? A common account views internal weight matrices as an associative memory. That account requires parameter counts that scale linearly with the number of facts. We develop a theoretical and empirical account of an alternative, \emph{geometric} form of memorization in which learned embeddings encode relational structure directly, and the MLP plays a qualitatively different role. In a controlled setting where a single-layer transformer memorizes random bijections from subjects to a shared attribute set, we prove that a logarithmic embedding dimension is sufficient. Subject embeddings encode \emph{linear superpositions} of their associated attribute vectors, and a small MLP acts as a relation-conditioned selector that extracts the relevant attribute from the embedding. We extend these results to the multi-hop setting---chains of relational queries such as ``Who is the mother of the wife of $x$?''---and provide constructions with and without chain-of-thought that exhibit a provable capacity--depth tradeoff, complemented by a matching information-theoretic lower bound. Empirically, gradient descent discovers solutions with the predicted structure. Causal experiments support the proposed mechanism: when subject embeddings are appropriately re-initialized, the frozen MLP transfers zero-shot to entirely new bijections, showing it has learned a generic selection mechanism rather than functioning as a key-value memory.

☆ Jailbreak Detection on Large Language Models via Contrastive Prototype Structures
Yan Qiu, Yuanyuan Li
Jailbreak attempts and harmful prompts remain a challenge for the safe deployment of large language models. We study whether contrastively learned representations can support safety detection without training an additional downstream classifier. We propose Jailbreak Detection with Prototype Inference (JDPI), which uses binary safety labels to construct harmful and benign prototypes and makes predictions from relative prototype distances. JDPI uses only binary safety labels for encoder adaptation, prototype construction, and validation. In our setup, it uses fewer labeled examples than the classifier baseline (Method A) by avoiding the separate split used to train the downstream classifier. Across four benchmarks covering different input formats, contrastive fine-tuning improves JDPI's detection accuracy by 9.1-18.0 percentage points. Using the same encoder, JDPI achieves slightly higher ROC-AUC on one benchmark and stays within 0.017 of the baseline across all four. With dataset-specific adaptation, JDPI exceeds the released Llama Guard 3-8B baseline by 0.173-0.310 in ROC-AUC across the four benchmarks. In multi-turn replay on TomGibbs, JDPI uses accumulated user history as the conversation unfolds, achieving higher conversation-level accuracy than when it uses the current turn alone. Across 12 directed transfers, JDPI outperforms the classifier by an average of 1.59 percentage points in accuracy, demonstrating stronger cross-dataset robustness.
☆ Who are you? The Personal Imitation Game
Muhammad Rizky Wellyanto, Hajara-Yasmin Isa, Kexin Xue, Vishal Ravichandran, Wenpeng Lu, Jerry O. Talton, Ranjitha Kumar
Large Language Models (LLMs) now perform strongly on Alan Turing's 1950 Imitation Game, so we propose a harder challenge, the Personal Imitation Game, which asks whether a machine can replicate a specific individual in conversation rather than a generic human. Testing with 30 participants, we establish a human baseline and report the identity signals interrogators rely on: stylometric features, personality traits, and biographical facts. To demonstrate the framework, we design an LLM-based system that plays the game for one of the paper's authors, drawing on an autoethnographic dataset spanning ten years of personal digital communication. In a 20-person deception study, interrogators choose our system over the author's own friends in 7 of 10 rounds, and the author over our system in 13 of 20; neither rate is distinguishable from chance at this sample size. We consider design improvements, applications of personal AI agents, and implications for identity and privacy.

☆ Ground, Iterate, Trace: Decomposing AI Research Ideation
Samson Yu, Qiran Zou, Jing Yu Lim, Rushi Shah, Anirudh Goyal, Dianbo Liu
Large language models have been widely used to generate plausible research ideas, yet it remains unclear what makes automated ideation effective and how generated novel proposals arise from prior scientific work. We introduce the Creative AI Researcher (CAR), an agentic framework combining literature grounding, iterative development and explicit conceptual operators. CAR constructs a semantic knowledge graph from retrieved literature and equips an ideation agent with graph-retrieval tools and four operators: bisociation, analogy, exploration and elaboration. Each operator call is checked against structural acceptance conditions and recorded, producing a trace of intermediate transformations and declared literature sources. We evaluate CAR with two language-model backbones across 40 LiveIdeaBench research topics and two execution benchmarks. CAR achieves significantly higher methodological novelty (MN) than single-shot prompting and The AI Scientist. Its methodological novelty does not differ significantly from the Research Ideation Agent, while its feasibility scores are higher on both backbones. With a shared experiment executor, CAR achieves the highest pairwise win rate among the evaluated AI methods on FML-bench-Lite and BioXArena. In a ten-condition ablation study, MN point estimates favor CAR over one-step proposal on both backbones, but no MN contrast survives correction for multiple comparisons. The results do not establish a consistent quality benefit from explicit operators beyond free-form iteration. CAR makes the sequence of operations and declared literature sources inspectable, separating judged idea quality from procedural traceability.

☆ Can Programs Replace LLM Judges? Executable Program Judges for Scalable and Efficient Relevance Labeling
Eugene Agichtein, Charles L. A. Clarke, Kaustubh Dhole, William Stern
Large language model (LLM) judges have become a common way to create large-scale relevance judgments for retrieval evaluation. However, each judgment requires a potentially expensive call to a frontier model, and the judgments are hard to interpret and may not be reproducible. In this paper, we ask whether an LLM can write a readable program for each topic that replaces its per-document judgments. To address these issues, we propose ExecJudges, a method for generating fast, inspectable and scalable judges for automatic relevance labeling. Specifically, ExecJudges uses a frontier LLM to turn assessment guidelines into executable programs, which rely only on lexical matching, standard NLP libraries like parsers, and pool statistics. An ExecJudges-L variant also uses examples of human labels if available. We evaluate ExecJudges on seven TREC collections by comparing the produced labels with trained NIST assessors, frontier LLM judges, and recently proposed per-topic classifiers. Our results show that ExecJudges agrees with NIST assessors at the level of the Claude Sonnet 5.5 LLM judges on TREC DL 2019-2021. With a small number of human labels per topic, the ExecJudges-L variant agrees with NIST more than per-topic classifiers with the same labels on all seven collections, and either exceeds the agreement of LLM judges provided the same labels or is within about 0.05 of it in Krippendorff's alpha. The labels rank the official TREC DL runs with Kendall's tau of 0.87 to 0.94, and writing the programs incurs a one-time cost of $0.17 to $0.49 per topic, with no additional costs for grading. Our results suggest that ExecJudges offers a reproducible, inspectable, efficient and cost-effective alternative to LLM judges for labeling deep result pools for system evaluation or ranking improvements.
☆ Vibe-Trading: Towards Agent-Native Trading
Haozhe Wu, Zhonghang Li, Chao Huang
Existing AI agents can retrieve information, call tools, run experiments, and produce multi-step outputs. Yet most are built around relatively static workflows and struggle to preserve reliable state as the environment changes. Financial trading makes this limitation especially consequential: prices, news, market conditions, portfolio positions, and available capital evolve continuously. A result is useful only when it remains tied to the correct time, data snapshot, and execution context. We present Vibe-Trading, a comprehensive agent-native trading framework and all-in-one workspace for building, evaluating, and evolving agents for financial trading. It unifies market research, financial data, strategy generation, backtesting, portfolio analysis, paper trading, multi-agent collaboration, and optional broker execution. Its central contribution is an architecture that keeps results, evidence, research history, and trading authority connected throughout the research-to-action process. Each result retains its source, temporal scope, configuration, and producing run. Shared evidence grounding enables verification, versioned research records support reproducible continuation, and an explicit authority gate separates strategy research from live execution. Together, these designs provide a practical foundation for trading agents whose results can be traced, reproduced, and acted on only under explicit authority.

☆ General progress substitutes for specific training data in language models
Mark Marosi, Peter Antal
Language models acquire their capabilities from training data, and developers withhold data during training to keep models from learning capabilities that could be misused. How long this delays a capability, and how much data is needed to acquire it later, is unknown. Here we show that the general progress a model makes on the rest of its training data substitutes for the data that was withheld. We withheld the training signal for individual skills in models of chess, Othello, molecular strings, bracket languages and web text. The delay of a withheld skill in larger models was predicted from smaller ones. A chess model never explicitly trained to respond to check ranked the legal responses almost as well as a model that was. When models were then trained on the withheld data, they acquired the skill with a small fraction of the data needed from initialization, and this fraction decreased as the models progressed. A control rule of equal difficulty was learned much more slowly. A programming language withheld from the training text of a 7-billion-parameter model was regained in the same way. Data filtering therefore delays a capability, and the data required to regain it decreases as models improve.
comment: 49 pages, 16 figures, 6 tables. Code: https://github.com/Mapika/progress-substitutes-data
☆ A Foundation Model to Measure and Predict Human Behaviour
Haoran Ye, Tianrui Huo, Hangzhen Li, Xin Zhang, Guojie Song
Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to infer psychological attributes, predict individual behaviour, and simulate human populations. However, despite generating plausible responses, they often misrepresent individuals and distort population statistics. Here we introduce Chiron, a foundation model to measure and predict human behaviour. We jointly fine-tune an LLM to represent psychological constructs, predict individual behaviour, and align population response distributions with human statistics. The training and evaluation data span 36 behavioural domains and 78 social science experiments, comprising 288,307 person records, 1,411,750 atomic behavioural decisions, and 99,953 psychometric assessments over 109 psychological constructs. In our evaluations, Chiron's psychological measurements agree more closely with human self-reports and exhibit fewer biases that undermine psychometric validity. It predicts individual behaviour more accurately than frontier LLMs (up to 28.4% relative improvement). In simulations of social science experiments, it more accurately captures the direction and magnitude of human experimental effects (82.2--94.3% lower effect-size estimation error). Together, these results establish Chiron as a unified computational instrument for measuring and predicting human behaviour.

☆ On-Policy Morphing into Hybrid Attention Models
Disen Lan, Xuanda Wang, Jianbin Zheng, Huafeng Kuang, Wei Tang, Yu Cheng
Hybrid attention models combine full and linear attention to achieve a better quality--efficiency trade-off. Converting pretrained Transformers into such models requires identifying which layers should retain full attention under a prescribed budget. We identify two off-policy mismatches in FlashMorph's layer selection: (i) continuous branch interpolation and teacher-forced hidden states differ from discrete hybrid computation; and (ii) frozen model weights fail to account for parameter adaptation during subsequent recovery. We introduce FlashMorph2, an on-policy layer-selection method that (I) evaluates sampled discrete hybrid architectures end to end using their own hidden states, and (II) jointly optimizes layer-selection scores and model weights under the recovery objective to reflect post-adaptation performance. Experiments across Qwen3 model scales and linear-attention variants show that FlashMorph2 consistently outperforms existing layer-selection methods, improving average scores over FlashMorph by $+2.42$ points on RULER, $+1.08$ on LongBench, and $+0.76$ on recall-intensive tasks, while completing layer selection in only $0.72$--$3.57$ GPU hours across 0.6B--8B models, demonstrating high efficiency and scalability.
☆ The Elephant in the Question: Unified Models Picture the Concepts Behind Their Answers
Tobias Braun, Marcus Rohrbach, Lorenzo Cavallaro
Visualizing intermediate representations could help us inspect the reasoning steps underlying a model output. Unified multimodal models offer this opportunity by combining understanding and generation in one architecture. Their image generators can access representations formed while processing text. We introduce the V-lens, which uses this connection to visualize a chosen token's hidden state without training a decoder. Across four diffusion-based and two purely autoregressive unified models, the resulting images reveal key intermediate concepts that connect a question to its answer even when the concepts are never named explicitly. Controlled tests show how this content changes with context, token position, and depth. We then introduce lens tuning, which supervises selected hidden states with pictures through the V-lens. Across BAGEL, SenseNova-U1, and HunyuanImage-3.0, the rate of correct visualizations of pronoun referents rises from 2-11% to 86-94%. Surprisingly, such visual supervision also improves text answers without supervising the answer tokens directly. Code is available at https://github.com/multimodal-ai-lab/V-lens.

☆ Induction Head Origins of SLAM-ASR Speech Recognition Models ICASSP
Artem Fedorchenko, Tanel Alumäe
What makes a small trained map from encoder states to the embedding space of a frozen language model sufficient for competitive speech recognition? The premise of the SLAM-ASR architecture is that a frozen language model possesses linguistic knowledge rich enough to perform the recognition task. This assumption, while reasonable, provides little insight into the mechanisms underlying these models. We therefore seek the concrete internal structures on which they rely. This work shows that SLAM-ASR models rely on induction heads of the frozen language model: the same heads that copy repeated token sequences in text are also important for mapping speech embeddings to transcript tokens. Moreover, we find pairs of heads that behave as a constrained form of differential attention, cancelling attention noise and sharpening attention onto the previously generated word. Finally, we show how induction heads could be used to improve transcription quality under noisy audio.

☆ DRIFT: Detachable Reasoning for Runtime-Flexible and Efficient End-to-End Driving
Emad Aghajanzadeh, Bethany Anne Wong, Leonhard Kunze, Moritz Thoma, Maximilian Forstenhäusler, Nael Fasfous, Manoj Rohit Vemparala, Pierpaolo Mori, Christos Anagnostopoulos, Daniel Mueller-Gritschneder, Ulf Schlichtmann
The integration of vision-language models (VLMs) into end-to-end (E2E) autonomous driving has demonstrated strong potential by providing scene description and reasoning capabilities. However, VLMs are computationally expensive, and switching language reasoning on and off at runtime without hurting closed-loop performance has not been demonstrated, which hinders practical adoption. To address these issues, we propose DRIFT, an E2E autonomous driving framework that integrates a lightweight language model (LM) for visual question answering and action commentary. By efficiently projecting intermediate driving representations into the language embedding space, we demonstrate that a compact LM (<150M parameters) can achieve strong scene description and action commentary. Furthermore, we introduce a runtime-flexible planning module that is trained on surrogate reasoning features distilled from the LM and can be conditioned on either the LM or the surrogate reasoning features at inference. This design enables bypassing the LM entirely when required by deployment constraints, without sacrificing driving performance. With only 255M parameters, DRIFT reaches up to a Driving Score (DS) of 97.03 and a Success Rate of 89.39% (mean over three runs) on Bench2Drive by interleaving its LM and surrogate reasoning at a chosen reasoning rate. This is on par with the strongest non-language models while being far more compact than existing VLM-based models. DRIFT further achieves competitive results on the real-world NAVSIM and AlpaSim benchmarks at significantly lower computational cost than existing VLM-based approaches. Project page: https://drift-e2e.github.io/

☆ Openhagen Worlds: Calibrated Environments for Measuring and Training Scientific Discovery Agents
Alexander Boesgaard Lorup
Agents that do science must choose informative experiments and reason from their results, but most discovery benchmarks cannot separate that skill from recall, unstated scoring rules or shortcut policies. We introduce Openhagen Worlds, a suite of 27 procedurally generated environments (some with finite truth spaces), from classic puzzles of experimental reasoning to simulator-grounded physics and chemistry, in which an agent runs experiments under a budget and commits an answer that a deterministic verifier checks against a hidden truth. Every observation states the complete scoring rule, and each of the 178 difficulty levels is calibrated: it is admitted only if, on 64 development instances, a scripted oracle succeeds on at least 0.75, the strongest plausible non-deductive policy on at most 0.25 and each gated shortcut on at most 0.35. The 92 core levels span all 27 families and are sized to train small models; the 86 frontier rungs extend the 15 discovery worlds with harder questions and with campaigns of three, five and eight or more committed stages on one hidden system. On the same 84 withheld frontier instances, one per family and rung, DeepSeek V4.1 Flash solves 32 at its default reasoning effort with 16,384 tokens per call and 57 at its maximum effort with up to 393,216 tokens per call (oracle: 79); the headroom that remains is largest in eight-stage campaigns. On-policy expert iteration gives a 9B model modest gains: its core-tier success on trained families rises from 0.090 to 0.148, partly through better action format, with no detectable loss on GPQA Diamond. A hosted service plays the core tier's withheld instances.

☆ When Does Continual Pre-Training Help? A Controlled Study of Dialectal Adaptation for Québec French EACL 2027
Eeham Khan, Richard Khoury, Leila Kosseim
Continual pre-training (CPT) is widely used to adapt pretrained language models to dialectal and non-standard language varieties, yet it remains unclear when this additional training is necessary, what drives its benefits, and whether aggregate evaluation scores adequately capture its effects. In this paper, we present a controlled study of CPT for Québec French language understanding across nine LLMs spanning different families and scales. Using a budget-matched protocol of 30M tokens per experiment, our results show that the benefit of CPT depends strongly on the starting model's existing competence in the target variety. When CPT is beneficial, the improvements are driven more by corpus composition than by data volume, with literary text accounting for most of the gain. Paired Québec and standard French evaluations further show that CPT primarily improves expression comprehension rather than grammatical competence, and that corpus register determines which regional expressions are acquired. These gains also involve capability-specific trade-offs, including declines in reading comprehension that aggregate scores can conceal. Overall, these findings show that CPT for dialectal adaptation should not be treated as a uniformly beneficial default. Instead, adaptation strategies should begin with a careful assessment of the base model's existing competence, accompanied by source-level data analysis and task-level evaluation.

☆ CurvDelta: Low-Curvature Delta Modeling For Surgical Learning and Unlearning
Zeguan Xiao, Zhuocheng Liang, Yun Chen, Yong Wang, Guanhua Chen, Pinyan Lu
A central challenge in adapting large language models (LLMs) is capability preservation: an adaptation can achieve its target while degrading general capabilities. We introduce CurvDelta, a scalable framework that incorporates the preservation constraint into the parameterization of the model update. By analyzing the local geometry of the preservation constraint around the pre-adaptation model, CurvDelta constructs a low-curvature space in which parameter updates leave the preservation loss nearly invariant. We make its construction practical for LLMs through Kronecker-factored approximate curvature (K-FAC) and a factorized projection. We also introduce CurvLoRA, a low-rank variant within the same preservation geometry, designed for more efficient adaptation. Both methods apply to preservation-constrained adaptation tasks such as capability learning and selective unlearning. We evaluate our methods on mathematical reasoning, code generation, and knowledge unlearning. For mathematical reasoning and code generation, our approaches achieve competitive downstream performance while better preserving general capabilities, offering a favorable acquisition--retention trade-off relative to the baselines. For knowledge unlearning, our main results on TOFU and MUSE show a better balance between targeted forgetting and knowledge retention than the baselines, with retention performance close to the from-scratch retraining reference.
☆ Piccolo: Co-Evolving Decision Models and Data through Recursive Self-Improvement
Zhenqing Ling, Qirui Jiao, Daoyuan Chen, Cheng Chen, Xinji Mai, Haowen Gao, Zhuo Zhang, Xuchen Pan, Mengsu Ding, Jiaji Deng, Yuexiang Xie, Xiaoming Qin, Weirui Kuang, Zhaoyang Liu, Yaliang Li, Bolin Ding, Jingren Zhou
We present Piccolo, a family of multimodal decision models (0.8B to 27B) that coevolves model capabilities and training data via Agentic Data Engineering and recursive self-improvement (RSI). The models predict probabilities over candidate answers for general-purpose text and visual judgments, agent harnesses, data workflows, and interactive games. Piccolo-27B tops the Decision Index Vision Full leaderboard with a score of 75.59, while Piccolo-9B and Piccolo-4B achieve 68.35 and 67.46, respectively. All three rank first on the Private benchmark in their respective model-size categories. Research agents analyze model behavior, construct training data, conduct experiments, and use the results to refine subsequent proposals. Semantic slices and a shared research memory connect these actions across the model family's development. With training recipes comprising hundreds of thousands of examples, data selection and feedback-driven refinement are central to the self-improvement process. Our large-scale RSI exploration yields three counter-intuitive insights for decision-model development: (1) Backbone-recipe co-adaptation outweighs raw model recency: earlier backbones can surpass newer generations on visual tasks under identical recipes; (2) Targeted data curation enables 4B-9B models to rival 27B baselines on specialized workflows; and (3) Continuous loss reduction can decouple from and even degrade discrete decision accuracy. Together with the released weights and tools from the Data-Juicer and AgentScope ecosystems, Piccolo provides an actionable blueprint for building frontier decision models and scaling data-driven RSI.

☆ Self-Rewarded Reinforcement Learning for Better-Structured Medical Explanations in Small Language Models
Prakhar Mishra, Bhavya Yajush Awasthi, Tanuja, Nishant
Small language models (SLMs) can run on commodity hardware and support privacy-preserving, on-premises clinical deployment, but their medical explanations are often short and poorly structured compared with large language models. ☆ Clover-35B-A3B-Preview Technical Report
DiffuSpace Technical Team
We introduce Clover-35B-A3B-Preview (Clover), a mixture-of-experts diffusion language model with 35 billion total parameters and 3 billion activated parameters, supporting thinking-mode generation. Clover adapts Gated Delta Networks to block diffusion by combining recurrent memory of previously generated context with bidirectional softmax attention inside the current block. Historical memory readout competes with current-block tokens in a shared softmax, with learnable block-size calibration and a separate, zero-initialized right-context convolution branch. Clover leads the seven evaluated agent metrics, including 73.6 on SWE-bench Verified and 51.7 on Terminal-Bench 2.1. On internal agent workloads, it reaches a reported generation speed of 680 tokens/s using one NVIDIA GB300 GPU at BF16 precision and batch size one. We organize the evaluation around model quality, drafting and verification costs, and deployment under constrained memory. We also describe a decision-model variant that generates answers to related questions in one denoising pass. On an internal set of 500 groups, joint prediction raises question accuracy from 90.37% to 93.88% and all-correct group accuracy from 65.4% to 77.0%.

☆ Transferring an analysis of human brain organization to language models
Donghyeok Lee, Sangwon Yu, Sangkwon Park, Hye Youn Park, Munyoung Chang, Sungroh Yoon
During human cortical development, functional systems become increasingly differentiated. We asked whether improved reasoning in language models involves a comparable regional reorganization. Applying diffusion-map embedding, used to characterize cortical organization, to matched base and reasoning-trained models from three families, we found broadly preserved gradient profiles with localized changes within a contiguous layer span. Grafting weights from these spans into instruction-tuned hosts improved mathematical reasoning on average, with gains rising with region overlap, while retaining more previously solved problems than grafts elsewhere. Across training, the span formed gradually during the early rise in performance, and grafts from intermediate stages helped. Grafts shifted most hosts partly towards their donors' behaviour and sometimes improved on problems the donors failed; the same analysis located a transferable span in a medical model. Together, these findings identify a reasoning-related region whose weights improve reasoning with low interference in the host's existing mathematical competence.

☆ Chain-of-Thought Hidden States Reveal Task-Solving Directions for Direct Answers
Houman Mehrafarin, Ioannis Konstas
Chain-of-Thought (CoT) prompting improves the performance of Large Language Models by eliciting intermediate reasoning steps. However, CoT is far from perfect: on some tasks, models perform worse than with direct-answer prompting, and on others they generate a few hundred tokens to reach the correct answer. We start by labelling each CoT hidden state as recovering if patching it into the direct prompt yields the correct answer, and failed otherwise. A linear probe shows that the model encodes these two classes differently. We then show that the representation space of the direct prompt holds a direction for each question that leads the model, in a short generation, to the correct answer more often than CoT does. Specifically, we rotate the direct prompt's last hidden state once, before generation, towards a direction built for each question from its own recovering and failed states. Across 25 tasks, these directions raise the direct prompt's mean accuracy from $46\%$ to $85\%$ on LLaMA 3.1 8B (CoT: $69\%$) and from $52\%$ to $86\%$ on Qwen 2.5 7B (CoT: $77\%$), with a tenth of CoT's tokens. CoT is thus not the shortest route to this accuracy, and these directions can be used in future work to train models to align their states with them.
☆ Large Language Models or AI Agents for English to Arabic Translation? An assessment on WikiEn-Ar190
Fatima Zahra Iguenfer, Yousra Chtouki
This research investigates whether a large language model or an autonomous AI Agent performs better at translating from English to Arabic, which is considered a low-resource language. The authors introduce WikiEn-Ar190, a new dataset of 190 carefully selected Wikipedia articles from 18 different areas, which were created and verified by two native Arabic speakers to ensure that they accurately reflect both meaning and style. These findings show that methods involving autonomous reasoning can improve translation consistency for language pairs with limited resources. Beyond the results, the approach offers a framework for evaluating and improving translations, potentially using them as assisting tools to expand the representation of low resource languages, such as Arabic, on the web.
☆ Late Entry in Looped Language Models
Ziqi Jin, Jiaxi Li, Weiwen Xu, Aixin Sun
Looped language models deepen reasoning at a compact parameter count by iterating a shared layer stack T times per token, turning recurrence depth directly into sequential decoding latency. Conventional early-exit decoding halts after initial passes without cache completion, truncating cached key-value states of past tokens to shallow depth. We study the opposite strategy, late entry, where new tokens skip initial passes and enter directly into the final K < T passes over a deep context cache. Once lightweight fine-tuning resolves hidden-state norm mismatch at the entry boundary, deep cached context substitutes for skipped token-local passes, allowing late entry to outperform matched early exit across model families and pass budgets. To turn this asymmetry into wall-clock speedups without stalling to build deep caches, candidate blocks verify through only the final K passes on the critical path while previously committed tokens complete passes 1 -> T alongside subsequent steps at zero extra serial calls. Projecting these finished T-pass wake states inside the existing output GEMM (Wake verification, governed by logit-margin tolerance tau in [0, inf]) unifies greedy-exact T-pass decoding (tau = 0, matching K = T block decoding within bf16 reduction order), margin-gated verification (tau > 0), and non-rollback lossy late entry (tau = inf) inside a single static CUDA graph. On Ouro-2.6B (T = 4), K = 2 Wake reaches 5.2x greedy-exact speedup at tau = 0 (+26% over K = 4 block decoding on GSM8K) and 6.8x at tau = inf (+16.9 points over trained early exit at matched speed). Scaling test-time recurrence across T in {8, 16, 32, 64} on Huginn-3.5B cuts K = T/4 rollbacks 21.5x with depth, driving greedy-exact (tau = 0) speedups to 6.1x (T = 32) and 8.4x (T = 64, +153% over K = 64 block) and tau = inf speedups to 10.6 to 12.5x.

☆ Objective-Specialized Residual Streams in Manifold-Constrained Hyper-Connections
Shantanu Agarwal, Ruchita V. Maitri, Zhiqi Huang, Vivek Datla, Alfy Samuel
Manifold-constrained Hyper-Connections (mHC) extend a Transformer's residual stream into multiple parallel streams with learned cross-stream connections. Prior work trains all streams toward a shared objective. We instead study whether assigning distinct learning objectives to different streams can improve task performance and induce objective-specific specialization. Using complementary lexical (sparse) and semantic (dense) retrieval objectives as a test case, we find that objective-specialized mHC consistently outperforms a non-specialized mHC model across the full sparse--dense fusion range, for both two- and four-stream models and at two model scales. We then examine how specialization changes the model internally using probes of stream representations, residual mixing, objective-specific gradient flow, and read/write gates. The specialized and non-specialized models behave similarly through most of the network but diverge in the final layers: the specialized model develops increasingly objective-specific gradient flow, distinct read/write routing patterns, and reduced cross-stream mixing, and streams assigned to different objectives become increasingly dissimilar while those trained toward the same objective remain highly similar. Fixing the residual mixing to the identity leaves the specialization benefit intact, indicating that the benefit stems from objective-specific read/write routing rather than learned cross-stream mixing. These results show that assigning distinct objectives to mHC streams can improve multi-objective retrieval while inducing structured, depth-dependent specialization in the residual computation.
☆ Do Frontier Model Ingredients Add Up? A Replicated Factorial Study of Small Language Models
Biswa Sengupta
Building a small language model requires choosing which published improvements to combine. We compare five changes in the same model, using the same data and training budget. We test selected combinations and repeat each one with a second random seed. Mixture-of-experts layers, Muon optimisation and a Gated DeltaNet hybrid give the largest gains. Extra normalisation also helps, while an attention gate with a matched parameter budget adds little on average. The gains mostly combine, but small interactions between changes appear again in the repeated runs. Repetition also reveals benefits that an initial statistical estimate treated as noise. These findings apply to our small-model training setting; the expert models also have greater total capacity. The practical lesson is to test promising components together and repeat the comparisons before deciding which ones to keep.
☆ ProbMedTOD: A Bayesian Network Guided Task-Oriented Dialogue System for Patient History Taking
Vishal Vivek Saley, Bhavesh Gurnani, Dinesh Raghu, Mausam
Patient history taking is a strategic, multi-turn process that refines diagnostic hypotheses through iterative questioning, mirroring clinical reasoning where each inquiry updates beliefs over candidate diagnoses. Deploying AI in this setting is constrained by privacy, cost, and latency, making Small Language Models (SLMs) a more desirable backbone compared to larger LLMs. However, SLMs capture only the fast, intuitive "System 1" side of this process and lack explicit mechanisms for the deliberative "System 2" reasoning needed to maintain and update diagnostic uncertainty over time. Bayesian Networks (BayesNets) offer a natural complement, providing an interpretable framework for auditable probabilistic belief tracking. Yet, clinical BayesNets are difficult to construct due to their reliance on expert curated structure, dependencies, and parameters. We introduce ProbMedTOD, which automatically synthesizes a clinical BayesNet from a small number of clinical notes in three stages: ontology building, structure prediction, and parameter estimation. ProbMedTOD then integrates the resulting BayesNet with SLMs, enabling probabilistic reasoning in multi-turn dialogues. A BayesNet Agent maintains real-time posterior distributions over diagnoses, while a Policy Agent picks diagnostically informative questions. Experiments on DDXPlus and MIMIC-IV Notes show that ProbMedTOD outperforms LLM-only and retrieval-based baselines, with especially strong gains for smaller models.
☆ MECI: Multi-Element Collaborative Interaction for Multimodal Entity Linking
Jie Peng, Yongxue Shan, Yongfu Zha, Xiaodong Wang
Multimodal Entity Linking (MEL) aims to disambiguate mentions in multimodal contexts by grounding them to specific entities in a knowledge base. A pivotal challenge in MEL is capturing multi-level correspondences: the semantic consistency between mention-entity pairs and the complementary correlations across modalities. However, existing methods often suffer from element dominance due to their reliance on coupled interactions or coarse global aggregations. In response, we propose the Multi-Element Collaborative Interaction (MECI) framework. First, to capture multi-element mention-entity correspondences, we develop a Multi-view Experts Network that leverages a "divide-and-conquer" strategy for decoupled feature learning to mitigate element dominance, supported by a KL-guided routing mechanism that governs expert specialization and collaboration. Furthermore, to model cross-modal complementary correlations, we propose a Hierarchical Multimodal Interaction Module, where a dynamic modality-aware weighting network refines interactions across hierarchical semantic levels, thereby integrating multi-granular evidence to counteract element dominance. Finally, we incorporate a generative semantic refinement stage that utilizes large language models for zero-shot re-ranking. Extensive experiments on WikiDiverse, RichpediaMEL, and WikiMEL show that MECI consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, improving Hits@1 by 1.95%, 7.30%, and 2.31%, respectively.

☆ Nothing to Declare: Steering a single neuron increases concealment in AI teams
Edoardo Chidichimo, Felix P. S. Wallis
Recent research suggests that individual neurons can have interpretable roles in AI behaviour. ☆ What Majority Labels Hide: Annotator Disagreement and Model Errors in Multi-Dialect Arabic Sexism Detection
Alaa Bazaid, Arkaitz Zubiaga
Judgements of sexism are subjective and may vary across communities, yet sexism detection datasets usually reduce annotator judgements to a single majority label, obscuring disagreement and how models behave on contested cases. We introduce MuDAS, a dataset of YouTube comments in Saudi, Egyptian and Moroccan Arabic, each labelled by three native speakers of its dialect, with the individual annotations preserved. We use it to evaluate Arabic encoders fine-tuned with hard-label, soft-label and perspectivist objectives, GPT-5.6 Terra, and four Arabic-centric large language models. Model errors are more frequent on comments with annotator disagreement. The Arabic-centric models also over-predict sexism and remain below the encoders. Disagreement-aware learning preserves Macro-F1, but soft labels improve alignment with annotators on contested comments at the expense of unanimous ones, and neither paradigm reliably identifies contested comments. Lower performance on Egyptian Arabic, clearest for the large language models, is not fully explained by the examined measures of test-set composition and dialectness. Majority-label scores can therefore hide contrasting error patterns that matter for content moderation.
