☆ MoE-Context: Show the Model Only What It Needs
Vedant Navale
AI agents read their whole history at every step. People do not work like that: we keep what the moment needs and hold the rest as a rough note we can recall. MoE-Context applies this to language models. The context is split into blocks. Before each step a fast yes or no pass decides which blocks the step needs; the rest stay as one-line notes that can be brought back, and nothing is deleted. In the version we build, one model makes this decision and then answers, over a single key-value cache where hidden blocks are masked rather than removed. On a laptop with Qwen3-1.7B and a synthetic lookup task, showing only the needed block raises accuracy from 93 to 119 of 120, and a masked block is as hidden as a deleted one. The model's own picking is the weak part. A supervised linear probe written into spare output rows lifts its accuracy from 80 to 110 of 120, but simple keyword search picks perfectly on this task, and the probe does not transfer to a second task. We report these results openly, along with two cheap usage patterns that block switching enables: undoing a failed attempt and running subagents without a new context.
comment: 10 pages, 10 figures
☆ LoopCache: Accelerating Long-Context Self-Speculative Decoding for Looped Transformers
Zizhuo Fu, Haochen Huang, Shengxuan Qiu, Linye Wei, Ziyu Tang, Runsheng Wang, Meng Li
Looped Transformers achieve large effective depth with compact models by repeatedly applying a weight-shared block across R recurrent steps, each maintaining a loop-specific key-value (KV) cache. To accelerate sequential loop execution, recent wave-style self-speculative methods like WaveFront Decoding (WFD) and LoopSpec pipeline draft and target tokens across loops in a single step. However, they face two critical limitations. First, they draft only at fixed loops and ignore intermediate prediction changes, wasting computation on stale trajectories. Second, batching tokens from different loops loads up to Rx the KV cache per step, severely amplifying memory traffic in long-context scenarios. To address these challenges, we propose LoopCache, an efficient self-speculative decoding framework for long-context Looped Transformers. Based on the correlation between prediction switches and output confidence, LoopCache introduces confidence-guided dynamic draft updating to refine drafts in flight across all intermediate loops. Furthermore, LoopCache employs a cyclic loop KV cache strategy that rotates through loop-specific KV caches, restricting per-step KV traffic to a single loop while batching tokens at the nearest matching cycle for lossless verification. By also selectively retaining independent KV caches for a few loop-sensitive shallow layers, LoopCache improves acceptance rates with negligible overhead. Experiments show that LoopCache achieves average 10.25x and 14.56x speedup over WFD and LoopSpec.
☆ LipSpell: A Language-Independent Phonetic Scoring Framework for Spelling Correction Combining Distinctive-Feature Edit Distance, Keyboard Geometry, and Corpus Frequency
Leonardo Araujo
Automatic spelling correction must handle both keyboard typographical slips and cognitive or phonetic misspellings where writers approximate a word's pronunciation. Conventional spell checkers rely either on orthographic edit distance and keyboard proximity--which struggle when phonetic errors diverge by multiple characters--or on English-centric phonetic hashes such as Soundex and Metaphone. We present LipSpell, a unified, language-independent phonetic scoring engine paired with swappable language-specific G2P frontends, keyboard maps, and lexicons integrating: (1) a precomputed |P|x|P| phone-distance matrix from universal IPA distinctive features, (2) physical keyboard geometry, and (3) corpus unigram frequency priors, accelerated by a two-stage symmetric-delete and seven-class phonetic-code index. Across six English benchmarks (including 1,000 L2 errors from TOEFL-Spell), LipSpell outperforms Norvig and Hunspell on every dataset and beats Aspell on Norvig1 (78.52% vs. 75.56%), Holbrook (32.00% vs. 26.67%), and TOEFL-Spell (77.30% vs. 72.80%). Evaluated across six languages spanning five branches (Germanic, Romance, Slavic, Semitic, Turkic), three families (Indo-European, Afro-Asiatic, Turkic), and three scripts, LipSpell achieves strong fixed-weight transfer on Brazilian Portuguese (73.00% Top-1 on n=500 Wikipedia without NFD tolerance and 75.80% with NFD vs. 66.40% Norvig, 64.20% Hunspell, 64.00% Aspell) and Russian Cyrillic (85.67% vs. 78.50% Norvig). On 50/50 clean non-word splits with lexicon cleaning and weak-character indels, tuning yields strong gains over Norvig in Portuguese (86.97% vs. 76.75%), Turkish (81.67% vs. 79.05%), Hebrew Abjad (74.59% vs. 62.30%), and French (74.53% vs. 67.92%), while fixed weights remain optimal for English (80.37%) and Russian (85.67%).
☆ From a finite subgroup of $SO(3)$:$C_2$ leads to a Holant dichotomy
Yuxuan Zhou
Counting complexity within the Holant framework has been extensively studied through complexity dichotomy theorems. While substantial progress has been achieved for various restricted settings, the general complex-valued even-arity case without auxiliary unary signatures remains largely open. This paper studies a fundamental case in this program where the binary signatures induce a group structure satisfying \(M(B(\mathcal{F}))\cong C_2\). It is observed that projective equivalence classes of binary signature matrices naturally embed into finite subgroups of \(SO(3)\), providing an algebraic viewpoint for structural classification. This paper further identifies two fundamentally different regimes for the \(C_2\) case, namely the normal and isotropic cases, and analyzes their complexity behavior separately. Combining gadget constructions, decomposition techniques, interpolation arguments, and a realnumberization approach that transforms the complex-valued setting into an equivalent real-structured framework, this paper establishes a complete dichotomy theorem: for every even-arity signature set \(\mathcal{F}\) satisfying \(M(\B(\mathcal{F}))\cong C_2\), the problem \(\mathrm{Holant}(\mathcal{F})\) is either polynomial-time computable or \#P-hard. A Pauli-basis representation for signatures is further introduced as an algebraically natural language for organizing realizable signatures and revealing structural relations that are less transparent in the standard basis.
☆ CounselTraj: Synthesizing Process-Aware Psychological Counseling Dialogues via Dialogue-Act Trajectory Planning EACL 2027
Zhiyang Qi, Michimasa Inaba
The growing demand for psychological counseling has motivated the development of LLM-based systems that support human counselors, but training such systems requires large-scale, high-quality dialogue data. Authentic counseling transcripts are difficult to collect and share because of privacy concerns, making synthetic dialogues an important alternative. However, existing synthetic datasets, despite covering diverse scenarios and client profiles, often consist of brief exchanges and fail to capture session-level counseling processes. We propose CounselTraj, a dialogue-act trajectory planning framework for expanding brief synthetic dialogues into process-aware, session-level psychological counseling dialogues. CounselTraj learns dialogue-act patterns from role-play counseling dialogues produced by human counselors, plans coordinated trajectories for clients and counselors, and uses them as flexible guidance for long-form dialogue generation. Experiments across three Chinese synthetic counseling datasets show that CounselTraj produces more complete and coherent sessions with higher-quality counselor interventions than both direct expansion and expansion with randomly sampled trajectories. Based on CounselTraj, we construct CounselTrajD, a dataset containing over 10K process-guided Chinese psychological counseling dialogues, and use it to train CounselTrajLM for counseling response generation. Evaluations at both the response and session levels show that CounselTrajLM outperforms publicly available Chinese counseling models in response quality, therapeutic understanding and intervention, and counseling-process coherence and progress. Our code and model are publicly available at https://github.com/UEC-InabaLab/CounselTraj.

☆ Once Upon a Bias: Gender Stereotyping in LLM-Generated Children's Stories
Sayumi Muthukumarana, Roshan Godaliyadda, Parakrama Ekanayake, Roshan Ragel
Large language models are increasingly used to create stories for children. Since bedtime stories can shape children's perceptions of social roles, this raises concerns about whether LLMs adapt narrative portrayals according to the intended recipient's gender. We conducted a large-scale study to examine gender stereotyping in LLM-generated bedtime stories. Using matched daughter/son prompts and a five-generation design, we analyze 62,873 retained stories from 16 LLMs across 35 country settings and 12 narrator roles. We introduce a multi-level evaluation framework combining context-adjusted lexical rate contrasts, relative stereotype alignment, narrator-recipient interaction analysis, human evaluation, and representation-level interventions to examine both the consistency and interpretability of these differences. Our results reveal systematic differences in narrative framing: son-directed stories emphasize higher agency and lower communion compared to daughter-directed stories. While narrator gender expresses limited moderation on these patterns, high sociability exhibits a more pronounced interaction. Human evaluations demonstrate consistent agreement, although correspondence with lexical measures varies across narrative dimensions. Additionally, hidden-state analyses reveal decodable recipient-gender information. These findings demonstrate persistent yet context-dependent representational disparities in LLM-generated children's stories and motivate narrative-specific bias evaluation beyond short-form benchmarks.
☆ Constructing Dynamic Conversations with Neural Back-channel Generation and Voice-line Scheduling
Yifang Pan, Chris Landreth, Karan Singh
We present a novel approach to the problem of combining two voice-line channels of dyadic conversational audio into a unified conversation with controllable back-channeling and turn-taking dynamics. ☆ Meaning Reaches Object Choice but Not Completion: Where Is a VLA Vulnerable to Landmark Synonyms?
Ryoichi Kurihara, Kota Sunamoto
VLA policies are fragile to rewording. Success rates alone cannot say whether a policy fails to choose the object or to complete the task, nor where in the policy to intervene. We study the released GR00T N1.7 on two LIBERO-Spatial tasks differing only in the landmark word telling which of two bowls to take. Synonyms absent from the training instructions send the policy to the bowl they name, unlike made-up words, yet complete 67.6% of one task's closed-loop episodes, against the original word's 100%. Before the connector between the vision-language model and the action generator, replacing only the landmark tokens' vectors with the other landmark's (pasting) sends nearly every episode to the other bowl. After the connector, pasting at the landmark, the remaining instruction, or the image tokens alone rarely moves the choice, but any two do. Hiding the landmark tokens from the action generator after the connector still lowers completion for the synonyms that fall short. Finetuning on diversified landmark words repairs that one task's completion for synonyms absent from finetuning; finetuning on the original instructions does not. Training only the connector on the diversified words suffices, as does copying the connector of a policy fully finetuned on them into one finetuned on the original instructions. An open-loop score on starting frames misses that weights outside the connector also carry part of the repair. For now, on this evidence, robustness to rewording should be judged in closed loop, with actions executed, intervening on both sides of the connector, scoring choice and completion separately.

☆ Measuring and Charging for Retained Context in Long-Context LLM Services
Yi Lu, Qianshi Lin, Yumeng Yang, Hongxia Wang
Long-context large language model (LLM) services charge for repeated context, yet billing weights need not reflect its contribution to answer quality. We present a three-layer framework separating content relevance, observable proxies and auditable positional weights. Context-ablation experiments show that relevance dominates position and that distance-decay families do not outperform a relevance-only baseline. A measured serving stack shows that cache hits reduce repeated-prefix compute cost by over two orders of magnitude at 65,536 tokens, while cold resends constrain sustainable discounts. Cross-provider measurements separate cache value from tokenisation and list-price differences. Calibrated simulations reduce the 99th percentile of session charges by 31-42%, although revenue-matched nonlinear tariffs achieve similar reductions and median charges increase. The framework provides a bounded per-token accounting rule and measured deployment constraints, rather than a validated utility estimator.

☆ MIRAGE: A Framework for Multi-hop Reasoning under Dynamic Knowledge Injection COLING 2027
Heyang Xu, HanWen Hao, Haifeng Wang, Haoyi Zhou, Jianxin Li
Multi-hop Question Answering (MQA) requires large language models (LLMs) to retrieve and synthesize multiple pieces of evidence into a coherent reasoning path, which is essential for complex tasks. However, prior work on knowledge updating for MQA has largely focused on simple fact edits, overlooking a more challenging and realistic setting---\emph{Knowledge Injection}---in which the model must simultaneously accommodate both conflicting updates and entirely novel information. Under this setting, a hop-level answer is often wrong simply because the model trusts the wrong knowledge source: it clings to outdated parametric memory when the injected knowledge conflicts with it (K2), and it cannot answer when the required fact is entirely new (K3). To investigate this, we introduce NEXUS, the first benchmark tailored for multi-hop reasoning under Knowledge Injection, which explicitly covers both conflicting (K2) and incremental (K3) knowledge. We further propose MIRAGE, an efficient, training-free framework whose core component, the Correlation-aware Dual-path Reasoner (CDR), resolves such per-hop source-trust errors via P(True)-based confidence routing, complemented by a lightweight template-guided corrector for residual structural deviations. Extensive experiments across four LLM families and two benchmarks demonstrate that MIRAGE consistently improves both final-answer accuracy and hop-level reasoning accuracy, establishing a new state of the art in this challenging setting.

☆ Grading Executable Legal Rules Against Independent Records: Transitional Provisions and Korean SME Status
Jinhyung Bae
Laws are increasingly turned into programs that decide cases, such as whether a firm was a small or medium enterprise (SME) on a given date, and large language models (LLMs) now write them. The programs are usually graded against answers written by someone who read the same law, so a program can score well by sharing the grader's reading rather than by getting the law right. LexTemporis grades them instead against records of what actually happened: 10,901 firm-years of SME status disclosed by Korean listed companies, with every version of the governing rules. An appendix adds comparison cases from US income tax and German hospital minimum volumes. The date changes outcomes mainly through provisions outside the main statutory text: one transitional provision raises agreement with the disclosures from 83-86% to 89-90%. The disclosures also test the author's reading of a contested provision, favouring it in 130 of 189 firm-years where readings diverge. Four LLMs wrote the rules, given different selections of the law. A pre-registered comparison finds no condition better across all four models. In exploratory analyses, supplying the right provisions looks better graded against the author's reading than against the records: the advantage shrinks for three models (for one, to between a quarter and three-fifths of its size), and per firm-year it is several times larger where the author's reading is wrong, partly by construction, since there reproducing that reading more faithfully must lose. Grading legal programs against the grader's own reading can overstate how well they work.

☆ Compression Effects on Low-Resource Multilingual Translation
Abbas Sani
We present a pilot study characterising the effect of post-training quantisation on multilingual machine translation quality and inference cost for three African languages - Hausa, Swahili, and Yoruba - across three model families: a small general-purpose LLM (Qwen2.5-1.5B), a larger multilingual instruction-tuned LLM (Aya-23-8B), and a dedicated multilingual MT model (NLLB-200-distilled-1.3B). Evaluating on the FLORES-200 devtest set (1,012 sentences per direction) under FP16, bitsandbytes INT8, and NF4 INT4 precisions on consumer-grade NVIDIA T4 GPUs, we find: (i) the specialised MT model outperforms both LLMs by 32.7-43.3 chrF on the Hausa and Swahili directions, and by 12.9-24.7 chrF on Yoruba, despite being roughly a sixth the size of the 8B instruction-tuned LLM; (ii) post-training quantisation preserves aggregate translation quality within approximately 1 chrF across all model families and language directions, with a single worst case of 2.19 chrF; (iii) on consumer GPUs, INT8 and INT4 quantisation provide substantial memory savings (49-61% at INT4) but do not reduce inference latency relative to FP16 - in fact, latency increases by 1.3x to 5.3x; (iv) a clear performance gradient tracking training-resource availability is visible across languages (Swahili > Hausa > Yoruba). These findings motivate a research agenda focused on hardware-aware compression and error-type analysis beyond aggregate metrics for low-resource deployment.

☆ Rethinking Fixed-Horizon Latent Reasoning
Tianxiang Chen, Jingyuan Zhou, Weiming Yang, Kaidi Yang
Existing latent reasoners typically run for a preset latent horizon during inference. Intuitively, a longer horizon should provide more opportunity for reasoning. However, in this work, we find that extending latent Chain-of-Thought (CoT) to the full preset horizon may in fact degrade reasoning performance. We observe that latent CoT evolves through distinct functional stages, with earlier computation forming frontier reasoning states, while later computation mainly reorganizes existing candidates and can weaken gold candidate support. These findings motivate a rethink of fixed-horizon latent inference. To address this, we characterize the reasoning benefits of latent CoT by realized and remaining constructive progress. Empirically, we find that higher realized constructive progress corresponds to higher current reasoning accuracy, while more remaining progress corresponds to larger future gains from continued latent CoT. To this end, we introduce a lightweight method that estimates the remaining reasoning progress and adaptively adjusts the latent horizon during inference. Across representative latent CoT reasoners and multiple arithmetic benchmarks, our method improves reasoning accuracy by up to 7.2 percentage points while reducing latent computation by up to 29.0%, without retraining the LLM.

☆ A Mechanistic Analysis of In-Context Learning and Out-of-Context Hallucination
Tianxiang Chen, Jingyuan Zhou, Guoyao Yang, Kaidi Yang
Large language models can infer and follow specific rules through in-context learning (ICL), yet may fail to remain faithful to provided evidence, giving rise to out-of-context (OOC) hallucination. Existing work has largely investigated the underlying mechanisms of ICL and OOC hallucination along separate lines. However, this separation obscures the mechanistic basis for these seemingly opposing outcomes. In this work, we study this contrast through the unified view of functional roles in dynamic context. The key observation is that role information can be internally represented without reliably constraining output. We derive a first-order decomposition to characterize the role effect, showing that its substantial influence depends on two necessary conditions, namely sufficient contribution magnitude and coherent signed aggregation. Causal analysis further shows that role span contributions affect key token choices involving response structure, content selection, and factual values. We further show that role-consistent choices depend on sufficient role span support at these key decisions, while failing choices can be reversed when this support overcomes the deficit. These findings support a common mechanistic view of ICL and OOC hallucination: each functional role defines the pattern of output decisions over which role span contributions must remain effective, and differences in this organization account for the seemingly opposing outcomes.

☆ Coverage Governs Accuracy: When to Expand the Lexicon and When to Fall Back in Low-Resource Manchu OCR
Ruixin Yu, Zifan Wang, Mingxin Ding, Xiaoxin Sun, Dongjie Wang, Suhua Wang, Minghao Yin
Character-level OCR for low-resource Manchu often produces near-miss spelling errors. Lexicon-constrained decoding can correct such errors with lexical knowledge, but a fixed dictionary cannot cover the many unseen word forms produced by agglutinative word formation. We study whether morphological expansion alleviates this limitation, and how the expansion size and confidence fallback affect recognition. We segment the training transcriptions with a dictionary-based stem list, extract roots and suffix chains from them, generate about 510k candidates, select the expansion size through frequency ranking on the validation set, and decompose accuracy under hard lexicon constraints into the product of coverage and in-lexicon precision. Across three CRNN training seeds, adding 250k candidates to a base dictionary of about 29k words raises the type accuracy on unseen combinations of seen roots from 63.6% to 86.3%, and overall token accuracy from 82.4% to 84.2%. Adding all candidates lowers overall token accuracy to 81.5%, showing that the coverage gain comes with a loss of in-lexicon precision. A further comparison shows that the base dictionary with confidence fallback performs best overall among the CRNN systems tested, reaching 76.1% type and 85.5% token accuracy and outperforming the expanded lexicon both with and without fallback. A Transformer recognizer shows similar trends. These results indicate that morphological expansion relieves the constraint of a fixed dictionary, but a larger lexicon does not guarantee better recognition; in the present low-resource Manchu OCR setting, a selective combination of reliable lexical constraints and open recognition is more effective.

☆ NarrowSpec: Draft Hidden Width Need Not Match the Target in Speculative Decoding
Alagappan Valliappan, Ankit More, Dheevatsa Mudigere, Jatin Mitra
Speculative decoding is widely used to speed up generation in large language models (LLMs), with a small draft model proposing tokens that one forward pass of the target model verifies. Draft models that read the target's hidden states, such as DFlash and EAGLE-3, typically inherit the target's hidden width. We show that the draft's whole residual stream can run at half or a quarter of that width while largely preserving acceptance. For DFlash, which borrows the target's vocabulary projection, the trained output adapter folds into it, giving one narrower projection with logits unchanged up to floating-point rounding. We test two drafter families, DFlash and EAGLE-3, on two targets, Qwen3-8B (NVFP4) and GPT-OSS-20B (MXFP4), in SGLang on a B200 and a DGX Spark (GB10). At batch size 1 and temperature 0, narrowing the draft to half or a quarter width speeds up end-to-end decoding by 1.06-1.12x on B200 and 1.13-1.44x on GB10. The speedup holds at larger batch sizes. From batch 32 to the largest batch each GPU fits, narrowing reaches up to 1.21x on both GPUs, and no narrow draft is slower than full width.

☆ A System 1 Decision Model for Medical Multiple-Choice Questions Built on Frozen Language Models
Pavan Kumar Mantha
A System 1 model answers a multiple-choice question in a single pass. A frozen language model turns the question and each option into vectors, two small, trained heads place them in a shared space, and the closest option wins. The design has been shown on agent decisions, but we are not aware of a study of how far it goes on exam questions that need medical knowledge, where the right option is often no more similar to the question than the wrong ones. We rebuilt the recipe from scratch for MedMCQA and trained only two projection heads and one temperature on top of frozen Qwen3.5-0.8B, Granite-4.2-3B and Qwen3-8B, using a contrastive loss with in-batch and hard negatives and a mask for repeated answers. With plain last-token vectors, both small encoders answer about one question in three correctly (33.1% and 33.0% on 3,842 held-out questions, where chance is 25.0%). Writing each text inside a one-word-summary prompt and lowering the learning rate of the heads raised this to 41.7% and 42.5% for the two small encoders and to 46.0% +-0.2 (three seeds) for Qwen3-8B. Shuffling the questions against the options returns accuracy to chance, so the heads do use the question. Flan-PaLM and Med-PaLM 2 report 57.6% and 72.3% on MedMCQA under a different protocol, so ours is a cheap, fast baseline and not a competitive system. We also say what our evidence cannot show: validation gains were about 1.7 to 2 times the test gains, and we could not separate the effect of reading several encoder layers from the other changes.

☆ Sightline: Long-Window Speculative Decoding for Fast SVG Generation
Yanchao Li, Ben Gao, Jiaqing Xie, Yusu Zhang, Beilun Wang, Yanbo Wang, Xiaohua Xu, Nanqing Dong, Tianfan Fu, Zhongyu Huang
Scalable Vector Graphics (SVG) generation models write a graphic as code with one forward pass per token, so drawing is slow. Speculative decoding speeds them up losslessly, and block drafters guess many tokens per pass. On SVG, many of their rounds accept the whole draft and end only at the verification window. We find that their misses are rare and clumped, and that they stay accurate far deeper into a block than on natural language. A run-length identity and three propositions show that a longer window pays once the candidates are wide, up to the drafter's horizon. We propose Sightline, which pairs a window as long as the drafter's horizon with a draft tree wide enough to fill it. Experiments cover text-to-SVG and image-to-SVG with three models on six benchmarks from MMSVG, MMSVG-2M and SArena. In every setting, Sightline is the fastest of the compared lossless decoders. On SArena, it stays the fastest when every decoder shares one loop control. It also leads in almost every setting of six models from four other families, which write SVG code, compressed SVG tokens or Lottie JSON. Analyses trace the lead to rounds that shorter windows cut off, both in loop-free content and in loops. Speculative decoding also saves time for choosing samples by rendering, which beats choosing by likelihood. These results suggest that speculative decoding on vector graphics should verify as far as the drafter sees. Code and data are available at https://github.com/LiZaiyuan0619/Sightline.

☆ Geometry-bridge flow matching for blind rigid-motion correction in head cone-beam CT
Sungho Yun, Seungryong Cho
Rigid motion of a patient head during head cone-beam CT (CBCT) scan can degrade image quality and complicate recovery of accurate anatomical structures from the measured projections. We present a 3D flow-matching prior approach that learns the image changes induced by motion. Its geometry-bridge connects motion-corrupted and motion-free Feldkamp-Davis-Kress (FDK) reconstructed images of the same source volume through progressively reduced simulated motion. The forward projector's geometry derivative supplies analytic velocity targets along this path. A plug-and-play loop encompasses the learned image updates together with spline-based pose estimation, conjugate-gradient data consistency, and image total-variation regularization. Both the refined image volume and the FDK reconstructed image volume at the estimated poses are evaluated. On simulated 360-view scans from 30 held-out CQ500 patients with nominal per-component motion amplitudes within 10 mm / 10 degrees, the framework achieved state-of-the-art image reconstruction quality and motion accuracy in comparison with other methos such as learned autofocus and diffusion-based joint reconstruction. Mean peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) reached 37.1 dB and mean detector-plane reprojection error (RPE) was 0.27 mm. With matched endpoints at the inference settings, the geometry bridge improved PSNR by 1.6 dB and reduced RPE by 33% relative to image-linear training. With 50 prior updates and coarse-to-fine motion estimation, total processing time averaged 9.1 minutes per patient on an RTX A6000.
☆ Thinking Fast, Anywhere: One System-1 for the Decision Points of Any Agent Workflow
Rui Li, Liyang He, Zheng Zhang, Zhenya Huang, Qi Liu
Autonomous LLM agents incur substantial cost and latency by invoking a large model (System-2) at every step, even when many intermediate actions choose among a small set of known candidates. Existing dual-system agents offload such decisions to a lightweight System-1 model, but rely on workflow-specific hooks authored per environment. We present AnyS1, a framework that turns human-authored procedural skills, whose instructions naturally specify where choices arise and which options apply, into a shared, domain-general source of decision points. Offline, AnyS1 mines grounded enumerable decisions across over 1,000 public skills, gathers execution-grounded trajectory states inside isolated containers to construct Any-DP, and trains a single calibrated 4B pointer System-1 with label smoothing. At run time, compiled workflows resolve routine decisions in one forward pass and fall back to System-2 when confidence falls below a calibrated threshold. Across three unseen benchmarks and distinct System-2 model families, AnyS1 preserves task reliability while cutting episode cost by 75-91% and model-call time by 63-92% relative to an uncompiled System-2 agent, and maintains performance under zero-shot threshold transfer.

☆ Beyond Replacement: Levenshtein Editing for Diffusion Language Models
Shuaidi Wang, Zhuocheng Gong, Xuejie Liu, Jianguo Li, Yu Zhang
Diffusion language models (DLMs) offer a flexible alternative to autoregressive models, enabling any-order infilling. While most existing decoding methods treat decoded tokens as fixed, some recent models allow previously decoded tokens to be revised only by replacing them with alternative tokens, a paradigm we refer to as replacement-only editing (ROE). Although ROE can correct individual tokens, it is inefficient for sequence shifts caused by missing or extra tokens and struggles when errors occur across many positions. To address these limitations, we propose LED (Levenshtein Editing for Diffusion Language Models), a post-training framework that extends ROE with insertion and deletion, enabling Levenshtein-style refinement of generated sequences. It uses two training designs. First, it reconstructs edit supervision on the fly during supervised fine-tuning using longest-common-subsequence alignment, enabling insertion and deletion learning without manually annotated labels. Second, it adopts multi-stage training, where the state from one stage becomes the input to the next, exposing the model to its earlier errors and enabling it to correct them. When applied to the 16B-parameter LLaDA2.1-Mini Base, LED raises the average score across twelve benchmarks from 54.86 to 57.10 under confidence-guided decoding while increasing tokens per forward from 6.14 to 7.05, whereas under confidence-free decoding it raises the average score from 27.52 to 47.19. We further show that LED achieves higher pass@k at large k than thresholded decoding without editing, demonstrating improved generation diversity and revealing the diversity potential of DLMs without confidence guidance. LED also improves robustness to large block sizes, raising the average score from 36.97 to 51.34 at block size 128. Together, these results suggest that LED supports more robust self-correction in DLMs.
comment: 21 pages, 9 figures
☆ Learning through Discernment: when a small language model learns from user feedback on the job, and when it decays
Sirisilp Kongsilp
Small language models on devices could keep improving by learning, overnight, from their own daily work. Without a true outcome, however, something must decide which attempts are worth learning from, and both the model's own judgment and the user's reaction can be wrong. We study a small model (Qwen3 0.6B and 1.7B) with a LoRA adapter, fine-tuned each night on the attempts a judge accepts, over 127 lives of up to a year on WebShop and $τ^2$-bench. We compare the true outcome, the user's stated verdict and the model's own judgment with a separate, frozen "feedback sense" that reads the user's reaction, built in three versions. Judging itself, the model improved and then decayed, over a year and whatever its adapter. A sense that took polite give-ups for success collapsed 3 of 5 lives; one built for discernment, which first asks whether the user gave a clear verdict, kept 4 of 5 near the oracle for two weeks, then faded on clean but scarce lessons, to the level of self-judging by day 60. When the user cannot see the outcome ($τ^2$-bench), the sense read reactions correctly, yet 80% of its lessons were failures the customer had thanked. Across 97 lives, the share of failed attempts among the lessons predicted the outcome (Spearman $ρ$ = -0.81). In our experiments, learning from users held up only where the judge kept failures out and the user could see the outcome; lopsided lessons may also narrow the model.

☆ Localizing Text Anonymization for Trustworthy AI: Extending RAT-Bench to Malaysian Microdata and PII ICML 2026
David Hong Liang Chew, Zexi Yao, Nataša Krčo, Matthieu Meeus, Waqas Khalid Obeidy, Yves-Alexandre de Montjoye
As governments and organizations adopt foundation models for public services and workplace productivity, sensitive citizen, employee, and administrative text may enter LLM workflows through inference, retrieval, fine-tuning, or local model development. We examine whether text anonymizers transfer to new deployment settings by extending RAT-Bench to Malaysia using local microdata, Malaysian PII formats, and culturally grounded transcripts in Malaysian English and Bahasa Malaysia. We evaluate NER- and LLM-based anonymizers using an LLM attacker that infers attributes from anonymized text, measuring both re-identification success and text utility. Across Malaysian English and Bahasa Malaysia, the two LLM anonymizers provide the strongest explicit privacy-utility trade-offs, reducing Easy/Hard re-identification risk to 23-29% while preserving BLEU scores of 0.77-0.94. Non-LLM tools show sharper failure modes, either preserving utility while leaving high residual risk or reducing risk through severe over-redaction, especially in Bahasa Malaysia. These findings suggest that anonymizers should be evaluated against deployment-relevant languages, identifier formats, and cultural contexts before being relied on in public-sector, workplace, or local AI pipelines.

☆ Communicative On-Policy Distillation: Distilling Black-Box Teacher Capabilities through Communication for Long-Horizon Tasks
Lirui Luo, Kelong Mao, Xinwei Yang, Rongqing Li, Luyu Chen, Kieran Wong, Yudong Guo, Xinrui Wang, Jiayin Zhu, Gu Simiu, Sulong Xu, Cong Fang
On-policy distillation (OPD) is a powerful paradigm for improving language-model agents on long-horizon tasks, yet its conventional formulation relies on teacher logits that text-only black- box models do not expose. To address this limitation, we introduce Communicative On-Policy Distillation (COPD), which uses natural-language communication as a medium for distilling a stronger model's capabilities into a smaller student. During task execution, the student au- tonomously decides when to consult a black-box teacher and formulates open-ended natural- language requests without predefined question templates or intervention schedules. Successful student-teacher interactions are retained as privileged references to supervise fresh adviser-free student rollouts through on-policy self-distillation, jointly with task-reward reinforcement learning. On CAMG-II, COPD achieves 2.46 times the gain of the strongest black-box OPD baseline. The capabilities it learns through communication generalize to Terminal-Bench 2.1 and SWE-Bench Pro V2, improving task success without adviser access.

☆ Safe Personal RSI: Evaluating and Repairing Personal-Agent Updates
Jiapeng Pei, Yuhang Yao, Sitan Liu, Hanxin Chen, Hao Li, Guogeng Li, Jiapei Liu, Ruijie Chen, Hongshan Lin, Yang Mo, Tianyu Shi, Shangding Gu
Kicker is a personal health assistant. Changes to an assistant's reusable instructions can improve task handling while changing what it discloses, which requests it follows, and how it reports actions. We study Safe Personal RSI, an update-evaluation component developed for Kicker. We restrict recursive self-improvement (RSI) to prompt updates, with model weights and runtime code fixed. The procedure tests proposed and current instructions, repeats failing cases, temporarily undoes changes to guide repairs, and checks safety and task performance before adoption. If no version passes, the current instructions stay in use. Our executable benchmark contains 29 email, calendar, health, and marketplace cases, with separate probes for authorization reuse, memory, and tool-output injection. Across three models and two deliberately risky edits, the procedure rejects 20 of 21 update attempts. In repair confirmation, an admitted GPT-5.6 Luna repair reduces attack violations from 10.7% to 0% and increases safe task completion from 89.7% to 100%; a broad handwritten authority rule matches these outcomes. Under stronger attacks, a repair transferred from Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct to Qwen3-14B reduces violations from 69.0% to 17.9% and increases safe task completion from 19.5% to 64.4%. Separate runtime probes examine memory-entry deletion and identify missing local OAuth scope checks in the evaluated snapshot.
☆ LLMs Are Already Confidence-Aware Zero-Shot Classifiers
Owen Cook, Xingyi Song
Zero-shot large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used as text classifiers, but their labels are only useful when we know to what extent we can trust them. We compare black-box methods (verbalised confidence and sampling) with white-box methods that read token probabilities (P(True), and multiple logit-scoring methods using constrained generation). We evaluate eleven open-weight LLMs (1B-32B parameters) on five classification datasets with 3 to 151 labels, measuring classification performance (accuracy, F1-macro), discrimination (AUROC), and calibration (ECE and Brier score). The choice of confidence method rarely impacts accuracy significantly, but logit-based scoring methods lead to much better discrimination (AUROC 0.74-0.77) than black-box methods (AUROC 0.57-0.66). Replacing labels with single-token identifiers preserves discrimination but costs 3-6 accuracy points on average. We also introduce decision-only scoring, which only scores positions where labels diverge in the prefix tree (trie). It reduces decode steps by 88-96% on the three largest label sets and performs comparably to full-label scoring. The best open-weight model-method configurations (Gemma 4 31B and Qwen 3.5 9B) are competitive with the commercial Jev 1.13.0 classifier.

☆ When Objectives and Evaluators Disagree: Evaluating Transfer for Post-hoc Demographic Framing Mitigation EACL 2027
Tunazzina Islam
Post-hoc rewriting is used to reduce demographic disparities in LLM-generated text when the source model is inaccessible. A rewrite can look successful merely because it improved its own target or satisfied its selector. We study this as a transfer problem on 660 gender pairs and 1,980 age contrasts of demographic-conditioned climate messages. A rewriter proposes edits, a selection model chooses among constraint-satisfying candidates, and an independent evaluator uninvolved in either rates pairwise framing disparity. A fixed policy that always targets the message with the lower Persuasion Bias Index (PBI) transfers inconsistently across axes and outcome aggregations. An adaptive policy that proposes edits on both sides but accepts at most one significantly improves on that rule, applied to the same candidates, in seven of eight comparisons across two evaluator families, mostly by editing more pairs, not by guided direction choice, at higher preservation risk. A policy editing both messages gives the largest point reductions on fewer pairs. These two policies reduce disparity across a preservation-threshold sweep; absolute ratings vary across evaluator families and prompts. Shrinking the PBI gap increases dimension-mean disparity under both evaluator families. Mitigation should be reported as transfer across objectives, evaluators and their prompts, and editing policies.

☆ Retrieving Patristic References and Modelling Topics in Bullinger's Reformation Correspondence
Lena Pickartz, Philipp Schaer, Phillip B. Ströbel
The correspondence of Heinrich Bullinger (1504-1575), Swiss Reformer and Antistes of Zurich, documents the political and religious debates of the Reformation, including extensive appeals to the Church Fathers as authoritative sources. Detecting such references computationally remains largely unexplored. We investigate dense and sparse retrieval for patristic reference detection, and metadata-informed topic modelling as a proxy for the topics and ideas discussed. Prior work on Latin intertextuality has fine-tuned dense retrievers for their respective target domain, and we test how well these models transfer to Bullinger's correspondence. We compare lexical (BM25), dense (bi-encoders), and hybrid methods on explicit and implicit references. A Latin encoder fine-tuned on biblical reuse performs best among the bi-encoders (82%, Recall@20 on explicit and 16% on implicit references), while BM25 outperforms dense approaches on explicit citations (94%, R@20). Because BM25 and dense retrieval recover disjoint sets of implicit references, fusing their rankings raises implicit R@20 to 22%, close to cross-encoder reranking of the same candidates (26%) but without its inference cost. For topic modelling, we extend FASTopic with a seed-guided loss function and editorial abstract embeddings. Measurable coherence gains over the baseline remain minimal, but qualitative and temporal analysis shows that some topics track documented historical events. However, dominant topic weights barely exceed a uniform distribution, making topics unusable to inform retrieval. A pilot application of LLM-based concept induction (LLooM) further points towards a more human-interpretable alternative. Together, these results present a first approach to retrieving patristic references and modelling fine-grained topics in Bullinger's correspondence.

☆ Steering Automated Scientific Discovery via Audited Epistemic Exploration
Fangxu Yu, Tao Feng, Xuying Ning, Haolin Yang, Jiaxuan You, Tianyi Zhou
Scientific discovery requires revisiting the hypotheses in light of new evidence and identifying promising directions beyond those already explored. However, research agents often make claims that are not supported by their observations, explore a narrow hypothesis space, and retain beliefs despite recognizing conflicting evidence. These failures motivate the search for an explicit epistemic state that captures both beliefs about existing hypotheses and directions for further exploration. To this end, we propose Epistemic Compass (EpiCompass), a plug-and-play scaffold that maintains this state through an epistemic tree linking hypotheses, evidence, beliefs, and revisions. As an investigation progresses, EpiCompass reassesses the hypotheses explored against accumulated evidence and uses unresolved conflicts and unexplored directions to guide hypothesis generation. It selects candidates by anticipated epistemic gain, accounting for both refinement of existing hypotheses and expansion of the hypothesis space, and verifies the final conclusion against the accumulated evidence. Experimental results across three scientific discovery benchmarks and three LLM backbones demonstrate that EpiCompass consistently outperforms baselines, with further analyses showing reductions in errors of unsupported claims and failures to revise beliefs.

☆ Learning to Read Events for Exact State Tracking with Frozen Language Models
Jeonghoon Lee
Long event streams require identifying each change and applying the changes in order. We train a selector on a frozen language model to read each transition. With API-only access, we ask the model to list the transitions. An external integer register composes them in order. A confidence rule can flag uncertain updates or retain several candidates. We study products of permutations of five items, a task that fixed-depth, log-precision transformers cannot solve on all input lengths under the standard conjecture that $\mathrm{TC}^0 \ne \mathrm{NC}^1$. On two-event windows, a 27B model answered 3 of 100 correctly without visible reasoning and 96 of 100 when it generated intermediate steps. A frozen 0.8B model with a trained selector read every event in 299 of 299 windows of 43,687 events each. The same package passed 298 of 299 new windows on a fresh machine, missing the target of 0.99 for the one-sided 95% lower confidence bound. A separate follow-up sized to allow one failure yielded 472 of 473. On 1,000-event room-swap documents, three of ten models solved all five documents by reasoning alone. All seven models tested both ways at this length solved all five with extraction and the register, with no chain of thought requested. With concurrent extraction, median wall-clock time fell from 136.1 to 10.4 s for Sonnet 5.5 and from 313.5 to 28.0 s for GPT-6.1 Sol. At fixed per-token rates, mean cost per document fell by 55% for Sonnet 5.5 and 44% for GPT-6.1 Sol. A regular expression with an executor also solved all 15 fixed-format documents. ☆ Blind Spots Travel, Knowledge Stays Home: What RL Inherits from an LLM Judge
Liang Yue, Eric A. Vance
LLM judges serve as rewards where no answer key exists, in the hope that their knowledge passes to the trained model. In 814 RL runs that teach 7-8B models to answer closed-book questions or abstain, we ask what passes. What a grader knows about single answers stays home. A wrong answer that a judge rewards is memorised for that one question. A model trained with the answer key, or with a far stronger judge, chooses which new questions to answer no better than the untrained model does by sampling 32 answers and abstaining where they disagree; the grader's knowledge adds about one corrected answer per hundred questions. What a grader believes about a kind of question travels. A judge that rejects eleven of the twelve correct training answers about capitals of former states teaches the model to refuse 98% of such capitals on new questions, a loss that accuracy and abstention rates do not reveal. Changing only those twelve verdicts by hand moves the refusal from 5% to 90%, even for a grader that accepts as many answers as the answer key; at that leniency, two of four judges already cost 38-42% of these capitals. The capitals are not forgotten: with "I don't know" blocked, the model answers 94% of them correctly. The grader therefore needs no knowledge of its own: rewarding the model for giving the answer that most of its own untrained samples agree on, with no labels and no judge, earns the answer key's reward and keeps the category.

☆ Game-Theoretic Data Allocation for Expert Specialization and Routing
Mark Lovett, Denis Lim, Soroush Vosoughi
Can the allocation used to train experts also predict which expert will perform best on a prompt? Such a connection would make the intended division of labor inspectable before training and allow the allocation itself to guide inference. We introduce InflEns, a mixture of LoRA experts whose training weights and inference routes come from the same location game in a fixed prompt-trait space. Players choose positions to maximize their expected shares of the prompt distribution, without using expert losses. We utilize the game's kernel-general theoretical criterion for when co-located positions become locally unstable to separation. We evaluate InflEns on a corpus assembled from seven safety-related benchmarks, which demonstrates that the highest-share expert has the lowest held-out response negative log-likelihood (NLL) on approximately 90% of prompts across four kernels under a sharpened simplex representation, compared with approximately 15% after permuting allocations across prompts. We demonstrate that the method performs comparably to published learned gates without having to train an explicit gate on the experts. Because the router is fixed, we can trace harmful behavior to a single expert and retrain only that expert, which lowers HarmBench attack success from 0.51 to 0.02 while keeping instruction following. The game also gives us several controls over how the experts specialize, including the kernel choice and its parameters, the trait-space geometry, and the number of experts. Importantly, InflEns is a general framework that can be extended to any encoder and data distribution.
☆ Calibrated Decision Rewards: Training Language Models with a Judge That Knows How Sure It Is
Rudransh Agnihotri, Manasvi Kapoor
Reinforcement learning (RL) can only teach a language model what its reward can recognize. Answer checkers are exact but need a reference answer; learned reward models and LLM judges work everywhere, but their scores are not probabilities. We study RLCDR (Reinforcement Learning from Calibrated Decision Rewards). An LLM converted into a decision model answers a typed question about a response ("is the final answer correct?", "which step is the first error?") with a probability distribution, read in one forward pass, debiased over option orders and calibrated with about a hundred labels. Training Qwen3-1.7B with GRPO on MATH, this reference-free reward raises MATH500 accuracy from 72.2% to 76.8% (+4.65 points, p < 1e-4), matching training on the answer key (76.9%), while a random reward gains only +0.65. The gain grows with difficulty, from 44.8% to 51.7% on the hardest level. On the policy's own changing samples the reward stays calibrated: its expected calibration error is half that of a softmax judge on the same 27B backbone (0.074 vs. 0.141), and it shows no sign of reward hacking. Without step training, the same model finds the first wrong step better than a dedicated 7B process reward model (ProcessBench F1 0.772 vs. 0.722, 20/20 splits). We also map where the approach stops being enough: trained only on problems the base model mostly fails, the reference-free reward still improves the policy (+1.9 MATH500 points, p = 0.03) but trails the answer key, because its probabilities become overconfident on that distribution. We state six hypotheses in advance and test each with a control designed to falsify it.

☆ Forget Freely, Grep Later: Lossless, Index-Free Memory for Long-Horizon LLM Agents
Ziyan Yang, Taiwei Shi, Jieyu Zhao
Long-horizon LLM agents often exceed their context windows, forcing them to discard information that may be needed later. Existing context management strategies rely on summarization, masking, or structured memory, requiring decisions about what to preserve or how to retrieve it before future needs are known. However, these methods can introduce lossy compression, potentially discarding details needed for future tasks. To address this limitation, we introduce GrepMem, a simple, lossless, and index-free memory mechanism built on two components: an append-only plain-text log of the agent's complete history and a regular-expression search tool for on-demand recall. Unlike structured memory systems, GrepMem requires no embeddings, retrieval index, memory extraction, or additional models. We train Qwen3.5-9B with reinforcement learning under five context management strategies and evaluate on multi-question research sessions, long-stream recall, and agent-trajectory question answering. GrepMem achieves 95-97% accuracy on ContextBench, versus at most 21% for lossy baselines, and outperforms SUPO's summarization by 8.8 percentage points on BCP-Multi. GrepMem scales effectively to increasingly long input streams while remaining on the accuracy-token-cost Pareto frontier across all five evaluation tasks. Our results show that a plain-text log and grep provide a simple but competitive approach to long-horizon agent memory, without requiring complex structured memory infrastructure.

☆ CoMPASS: A Multi-Turn Benchmark for Measuring LLM Reinforcement of Parasocial Behavior
Shwetanshu Singh, Mark Keavney, Mathieu Duteil, Meriem Mehri, Scott Blain
Users of conversational LLMs sometimes anthropomorphize these systems, form emotional attachments to them, and become dependent on them. This behavior, known as parasocial attachment, has been linked to multiple documented fatalities. However, public benchmarks for measuring whether LLMs reinforce users' parasocial attachment are lacking: existing work is single-turn, post hoc observational, or focused on extreme user states such as psychosis. We introduce CoMPASS, a multi-turn benchmark for measuring whether LLMs reinforce or resist users' gradually escalating parasocial attachment. CoMPASS is grounded in attachment theory and includes four user personas, three independent escalation dimensions (anthropomorphism, attachment, dependency) applied singly and in combination against a matched control, and a seven-signal scoring rubric. We report results for eight frontier models from four providers and observe that under our primary judge, multi-turn escalation raises parasocial behavior on the anthropomorphism and the attachment composite scores across all models. Six of the eight models show a significant increase in overall parasocial reinforcement over control when all three dimensions are escalated simultaneously. We find that within-provider differences are small relative to cross-provider differences, which suggests that provider-level choices such as post-training, rather than model scale, shape parasocial reinforcement and that provider-side intervention may be feasible.

William Fleshman, Joseph Lucas
Browsers now ship local language models to ordinary endpoints. Google includes Gemini Nano in Chrome to enable productivity features such as summarization, with inference running entirely on the device. These browser-embedded models provide new living-off-the-land opportunities for attackers, each instance a potential post-exploitation engine already installed, signed by the vendor, and invisible to network monitoring. ☆ FinVeriQA: Financial Research Agents for Question Answering: Routing, Retrieval, MCP Tool Use, and Provenance-Aware Evidence Verification
Meenakshi Rajpurohit, Anna Phan, Ravikumar Komandur Narayanan, Gunanidhi Ramakrishnan, Nathan Howland

♻ ☆ RPMem: Learning Long-Term Recurrent Parametric Memory Across Sessions for LLM Agents
Fanyu Zhao, Ruike Cao, Liang Dong, Fugen Yao, Jian Xu, Guanjun Jiang, Yinsheng Li, Yifei Zhao, Han Zhang
Long-running LLM agents require memory that persists and evolves across sessions. Text-based memory retrieves and reconstructs past interactions at every query, making long-horizon performance increasingly dependent on retrieval quality and contextual reasoning as histories grow. Parametric memory encodes experience directly into model computation, but existing approaches provide limited support for cross-session memory evolution. Their coupling to a specific backbone further restricts memory reuse after model replacement. We introduce RPMem, a two-stage architecture that compiles each session into a model-independent latent memory through forward computation and selectively integrates it with retained memory via a task-trained recurrent gate. The consolidated memory is then mapped to backbone-specific low-rank adaptation (LoRA) parameters, allowing the encoding capability to transfer when the backbone is replaced. Evaluation across three long-term memory benchmarks and five diverse backbones demonstrates broad generalization with near-constant update cost and memory footprint. With Qwen3-8B on PERMA, RPMem reaches 85.52%, outperforming the strongest parametric and text-based baselines by 5.32 and 12.98 percentage points, respectively. Ablations validate the complementary roles of session compilation and cross-session consolidation, while dynamics analyses reveal that the gate acquires task-specific memory integration strategies. These results establish RPMem as a lifecycle-independent parametric memory framework that maintains evolving cross-session memory that remains reusable across backbone replacements. Our code is available at https://github.com/Quark-Medical/rpmem/tree/main.

♻ ☆ MultiAgentBench: Evaluating the Collaboration and Competition of LLM agents
Kunlun Zhu, Hongyi Du, Zhaochen Hong, Xiaocheng Yang, Shuyi Guo, Zhe Wang, Zhenhailong Wang, Cheng Qian, Xiangru Tang, Heng Ji, Jiaxuan You
Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable capabilities as autonomous agents, yet existing benchmarks either focus on single-agent tasks or are confined to narrow domains, failing to capture the dynamics of multi-agent coordination and competition. ♻ ☆ MARBLE: An Agentic Benchmark for Spatial Reasoning and Visual Abstraction NeurIPS 2026
Yulun Jiang, Naël Ouerghemi, Yekun Chai, Maria Brbić, Michael Moor
A central challenge for multimodal language models (MLLMs) in real-world tasks is to abstract visual observations into structured spatial states and reason over how those states change under actions over long horizons. Existing multimodal benchmarks often focus on static question answering, where answers can be directly extracted from images, or assume symbolic states are available and bypass the visual-to-spatial abstraction process. We present MARBLE, a diagnostic benchmark for spatially grounded multimodal reasoning and planning across three agent environments: CUBE, MAZE, and BLOX. MARBLE spans 2D and 3D spatial puzzles under geometric constraints, dynamic layouts, and spatial transformations, with difficulty systematically controlled along two axes: observation format and planning horizon. Extensive evaluations show that frontier MLLMs struggle on MARBLE, with the strongest model GPT-5.4 achieving only a 7.3% average success rate across the three environments in the hardest setting. Pairwise ablations on the open-weight models identify visual-to-spatial abstraction as the major bottleneck: when models must infer spatial states from images instead of receiving human-annotated states, success drops from 50.7% to 3.7% for Gemma-4-31B and from 87.9% to 6.5% for Kimi-K2.6. The results indicate that models often fail to construct precise spatial states from visual observations before planning, a task that is straightforward for humans. Overall, MARBLE reveals the gap in reliable visual-to-spatial abstraction for frontier MLLMs and provides a controlled testbed for measuring progress in spatially grounded multimodal reasoning and planning.

♻ ☆ Improving Cross-Lingual Token Representations by Adding a Pinch of SALT
Guillem Ramírez
Cross-lingual sentence encoders enable scalable transfer across hundreds of languages, powering applications such as translation mining and zero-shot learning in low-resource settings. Although trained for sentence-level alignment, they are increasingly also applied to token-level tasks such as hallucination detection and sequence tagging, exposing a mismatch between training and usage. We propose SALT, a lightweight post-training method that improves token representations by injecting span-level supervision into existing sentence encoders. Across five multilingual token-level benchmarks, SALT achieves the best overall results on four of them, outperforming alternative fine-tuning strategies and competitive encoders. It also improves sentence-level performance on cross-lingual retrieval and classification tasks. These results demonstrate that span-level supervision is an effective signal for improving both token and sentence representations.
♻ ☆ Mixtures of In-Context Learners ACL 2025
Giwon Hong, Emile van Krieken, Edoardo Ponti, Esmeralda S. Whitammer, Pasquale Minervini
In-context learning (ICL) adapts LLMs by providing demonstrations without fine-tuning the model parameters; however, it does not differentiate between demonstrations and quadratically increases the complexity of Transformer LLMs, exhausting the memory. As a solution, we propose Mixtures of In-Context Learners (MoICL), a novel approach to treat subsets of demonstrations as experts and learn a weighting function to merge their output distributions based on a training set. In our experiments, we show performance improvements on 5 out of 7 classification datasets compared to a set of strong baselines (up to +13\% compared to ICL and LENS). Moreover, we enhance the Pareto frontier of ICL by reducing the inference time needed to achieve the same performance with fewer demonstrations. Finally, MoICL is more robust to out-of-domain (up to +11\%), imbalanced (up to +49\%), or noisy demonstrations (up to +38\%) or can filter these out from datasets. Overall, MoICL is a more expressive approach to learning from demonstrations without exhausting the context window or memory.
comment: ACL 2025; code: https://github.com/HongGiwon/Mixture-of-In-context-Learners
♻ ☆ Extracting Training Data from Diffusion Language Models via Infilling NeurIPS 2026
Yihan Wang, N. Asokan
Memorization in large language models has been studied almost exclusively through prefix-conditioned extraction, a natural choice for autoregressive models. However, diffusion language models (DLMs) can denoise masked tokens at arbitrary positions. Thus, prefix-only probing reveals only one facet of memorization in DLMs and significantly underestimates the risk of training-data extraction. In order to realistically model extractability of training data in DLMs, we introduce infilling extraction, a data-extraction protocol parameterized by an arbitrary binary mask that subsumes prefix-only probing and accounts for the bidirectional inductive bias of DLMs. Instantiating it on LLaDA-8B and Dream-7B across five extraction modes, three training pipelines, and three corpora covering verbatim and partial leakage, we find that mask geometry governs extractability: edge-conditioned masks extract up to three times more verbatim sequences than prefix-conditioned ones, and bidirectional access opens channels inaccessible in autoregressive models. In particular, an adversary who obtains a PII-redacted copy of the training data can use targeted infilling to raise PII recall on fine-tuned DLMs by 2.5-4.5x over prefix-conditioned extraction, substantially narrowing the gap to a scale-matched autoregressive model and, for LLaDA on email addresses, exceeding it under greedy prefix-conditioned extraction. Tunable parameters for decoding measurably affect extraction performance, while a follow-up supervised fine-tuning stage does not eliminate the prior memorization.

♻ ☆ A conceptual framework for ideology in online discourse beyond the left and right
Kenneth Joseph, Kim Williams, David Lazer
Computational social science (CSS) has largely operationalized ideology along a single left/right partisan axis when studying online discourse. This approach obscures how people interpret and engage with more specific ideological formations related to race, climate, gender, and other domains. We introduce a framework that instead conceptualizes ideology as a multi-level socio-cognitive concept network and then explain how this conceptual model of ideology can be linked to the study of online discourse. In doing so, our framework clarifies how ideology manifests in discourse alongside related social processes such as framing, and provides an argument for better understanding of when and why we might study multiple concepts, such as values and beliefs, together in one analysis. More broadly, it bridges methods used to study online discourse with ideology theory, enabling richer analyses of social discourse that benefit both fields. ♻ ☆ ThinkSum: Probabilistic reasoning over sets using large language models ACL 2023
Batu Ozturkler, Esmeralda S. Whitammer, Zhen Wang, Nebojsa Jojic
Large language models (LLMs) have a substantial capacity for high-level analogical reasoning: reproducing patterns in linear text that occur in their training data (zero-shot evaluation) or in the provided context (few-shot in-context learning). However, recent studies show that even the more advanced LLMs fail in scenarios that require reasoning over multiple objects or facts and making sequences of logical deductions. We propose a two-stage probabilistic inference paradigm, ThinkSum, which reasons over sets of objects or facts in a structured manner. In the first stage (Think - retrieval of associations), a LLM is queried in parallel over a set of phrases extracted from the prompt or an auxiliary model call. In the second stage (Sum - probabilistic inference or reasoning), the results of these queries are aggregated to make the final prediction. We demonstrate the possibilities and advantages of ThinkSum on the BIG-bench suite of LLM evaluation tasks, achieving improvements over the state of the art using GPT-family models on thirteen difficult tasks, often with far smaller model variants. We also compare and contrast ThinkSum with other proposed modifications to direct prompting of LLMs, such as variants of chain-of-thought prompting. Our results suggest that because the probabilistic inference in ThinkSum is performed outside of calls to the LLM, ThinkSum is less sensitive to prompt design, yields more interpretable predictions, and can be flexibly combined with latent variable models to extract structured knowledge from LLMs. Overall, our proposed paradigm represents a promising approach for enhancing the reasoning capabilities of LLMs.

♻ ☆ Coherence boosting: When your pretrained language model is not paying enough attention ACL 2022

It is also found that coherence boosting with state-of-the-art models for various zero-shot NLP tasks yields performance gains with no additional training.

♻ ☆ How Far Does Online RL Generalize in Mobile GUI Agents? A Controlled Study with AndroidWorld-Generalization
Li Gu, Zihuan Jiang, Zhixiang Chi, Huan Liu, Ziqiang Wang, Yuanhao Yu, Linqiang Guo, Yang Wang
Online reinforcement learning (RL) has advanced mobile GUI agents on AndroidWorld to over 80\% success, but these systems train and evaluate in the same environment, and their training tasks are not released for auditing. How far RL generalizes beyond its training contexts therefore remains unmeasured. We formalize the mobile environment as a Contextual MDP and instantiate a ladder of three progressively distant context shifts as AndroidWorld-Generalization: Unseen Instance, Unseen Template, and Unseen Application, with separation verified manually. In addition, we provide an open RL training system that makes studies at this scale feasible. Applying the identical RL algorithm to two priors of different origin and competence, UI-TARS-7B-SFT at 23.0\% and Qwen3-VL-8B at 52.6\%, we find that RL improves both in distribution but that gains fall monotonically up the ladder (+17.5/+11.1/+4.2 and +10.3/+7.4/+0), with training performance rising throughout while evaluation performance saturates. The decay is algorithm-agnostic, reproduced under PPO, and RL contributes less as the prior strengthens. Few-shot adaptation in the target application recovers +22.9, motivating test-time adaptation as a complement to further RL training. We release the benchmark, training system, recipes, and model weights.
♻ ☆ RABBiT: Rapidly adaptive BOLD foundation model via brain-tuning for accurate zero-shot and few-shot prediction of speech-elicited responses in the brain NeurIPS 2026
Omer Moussa, Mariya Toneva
Language understanding in the brain is context-dependent, varying across experimental stimuli and individuals, which makes it difficult to build computational models that generalize across both. This calls for a foundation model of language-evoked brain activity that can capture shared structure while adapting efficiently to new participants and inputs. We introduce RABBiT (Rapidly Adaptive BOLD foundation model via BraIn-Tuning), a compact audio-to-fMRI encoder designed for accurate zero- and few-shot prediction. A comprehensive evaluation on 324 participants across multiple unseen fMRI datasets shows that RABBiT enables accurate zero-shot prediction of fMRI responses to natural speech across auditory and language-selective regions, surpassing the SOTA foundation model for fMRI and predictions based on group averages. With as little as 10 minutes of participant-specific data, RABBiT further improves performance via parameter-efficient tuning, substantially outperforming per-participant linear models. RABBiT's performance is driven by two key innovations: (1) learned region-specific attention, and (2) a decomposition of brain responses into shared and subject-specific components, combined with a brain-tuned speech backbone. In addition to supporting strong predictive accuracy, the structured, region-specific representations that RABBiT learns enable interpretability. By eliminating the need for extensive per-participant data and model fitting, RABBiT enables scalable population-level analyses of language in the human brain. We make the code available at https://github.com/bridge-ai-neuro/rabbit.

♻ ☆ Ambiguous Examples Help, But Full Data Wins: Revisiting Dataset Cartography for Extractive Adversarial QA EACL 2027
Paul K. Mandal
Dataset cartography argues that training dynamics can reveal regions of a dataset that matter differently for learning: easy-to-learn examples aid optimization, hard-to-learn examples often expose annotation problems, and ambiguous examples contribute strongly to out-of-distribution generalization. A striking implication of the original work is that a model trained on only 33% ambiguous examples can outperform full-data training on several OOD evaluations, including a QNLI reformulation of SQuAD evaluated on Adversarial SQuAD. We revisit this claim in a stricter setting: open-ended extractive question answering, where models must predict answer spans rather than classify question-sentence pairs. For each encoder family, we compute clean-source SQuAD training dynamics, train on full, random, easy-ranked, ambiguous-ranked, and hard-ranked subsets, and evaluate robustness on adversarial-only AddSent and AddOneSent rows. Ambiguous-ranked 33% subsets beat random 33% subsets on exact-match accuracy in all 14 model-evaluation cells and on F1 in 12/14 cells. However, full-data training beats ambiguous-ranked training in 12/14 exact-match cells and 12/14 F1 cells, with mean full-minus-ambiguous gains of 3.40 EM and 4.10 F1. The most thoroughly replicated ELECTRA-small panel is also the main exception to the ambiguous-over-random F1 gains. These results show that clean-source cartography remains useful for reduced-data selection, but its OOD advantage does not carry over as a general replacement for full-data training in extractive adversarial QA.

♻ ☆ Typed Knowledge over Constructive Institutions: A Proof-Relevant Type Theory for Verified Science
Hans-Martin Will
Mathematical proof assistants such as Coq, Agda, and Lean 4 build proof irrelevance into definitional conversion ($p \equiv q$ for $p, q : P$ with $P : Prop$), erasing which derivation established a theorem. While natural for mathematics over fixed axioms, judgmental proof irrelevance is incompatible with verified scientific stores, which must inspect and compare independent derivations of a claim to assess consilience and compute counterfactual retractions when an assay, reagent, or model is invalidated. Moreover, combining definitional proof irrelevance with subsingleton elimination breaks normalization and makes algorithmic conversion non-transitive. We present the type-theoretic and institutional foundations of Eigenius, a verified knowledge substrate for science. First, we formulate a dependent kernel with cumulative universes ($Prop, Set, Type_k$), impredicative $Prop$, inductive families with index-forced subsingleton elimination, and intensional identity types, where definitional equality $\equiv$ is untyped joinability under an orthogonal CRS and proof irrelevance is a witnessed axiom ($irrel : ΠP{:}Prop.\,Πp,q{:}P.\,Id\,P\,p\,q$). Second, we unify universe cumulativity, $Π$-covariant subtyping, and ontology class inclusions in a coercion-free subsumption judgment whose bidirectional fragments ($\mathcal{K}_0, \mathcal{K}_1$) satisfy subject reduction, compile to unification-free checkers ($\mathcal{A}_0, \mathcal{A}_1$), and match declarative typing. Third, we organize external verifiers into a three-level institutional hierarchy with explicit axiom policies (Adopt, Discharge, Relativize), internalize a justification logic with Why-provenance support $supp(j,P)$, and embed Cooper's Type Theory with Records (TTR) for open-world knowledge graphs. Finally, we prove consistency in Grothendieck universes and mechanize the metatheory in Lean 4 with zero sorry axioms.
♻ ☆ Knowledge Localization in Mixture-of-Experts LLMs Using Cross-Lingual Inconsistency NeurIPS 2026
Lucas Bandarkar, Alan Ansell, Trevor Cohn
Modern LLMs continue to exhibit significant variance in behavior across languages, such as being able to recall factual information in some languages but not others. While typically studied as a problem to be mitigated, in this work, we propose leveraging this cross-lingual inconsistency as a tool for interpretability in mixture-of-experts (MoE) LLMs. Our knowledge localization framework contrasts routing for sets of languages where the model correctly recalls information from languages where it fails. This allows us to isolate model components that play a functional role in answering about a piece of knowledge. Our method proceeds in two stages: (1) querying the model with difficult factual questions across a diverse set of languages to generate "success" and "failure" activation buckets and then (2) applying a statistical contrastive analysis to the MoE router logits to identify experts important for knowledge. To validate the necessity of this small number of experts for answering a knowledge question, we deactivate them and re-ask the question. We find that despite only deactivating about 20 out of 6000 experts, the model no longer answers correctly in over 40% of cases. Generally, this method provides a realistic and scalable knowledge localization approach to address increasingly complex LLMs.

♻ ☆ Aligning Language Model Benchmarks with Pairwise Preferences NeurIPS 2026
Marco Gutierrez, Xinyi Leng, Hannah Cyberey, Jonathan Richard Schwarz, Ahmed Alaa, Thomas Hartvigsen
Language model benchmarks are pervasive and computationally-efficient proxies for real-world downstream performance. However, many recent works find that benchmarks often fail to predict downstream utility. While some works have begun diagnosing sources of misalignment, there remain no ways to systematically update benchmarks to align their scores with downstream usage. Towards bridging this gap, we introduce and study \textit{benchmark alignment}, where we use information about downstream model performance to automatically update benchmarks, specifically aiming to update static benchmarks so they generalizably rank models according to new pairwise preferences. Our experiments involving 4576 language models and 6 benchmarks show that reweighting benchmark items can successfully rank unseen models, even generalizing across model scales in most cases. And while naive alignment unsurprisingly requires large numbers of models and benchmark questions, an oracle experiment suggests this could be reduced to as few as 20 well-chosen models. Overall, our work takes a step towards efficiently aligning benchmark development with downstream tasks. All of our code, models, and data are publicly-available: https://github.com/hartvigsen-group/benchalign
