♻ ☆ Harness-Zero: Harness Distillation via Agent-as-Harness
Haoran Ye, Yuxing Lu, Haonan Dong, Zhaochen Su, Guojie Song
Agent harnesses, the external systems that mediate model-environment interaction, can substantially improve agent performance, but their gains remain tied to the harness at deployment. Because the best harness varies across domains, instances, and models, a general-purpose agent must either settle for a suboptimal shared harness or route among an ever-growing set of specialized ones. We therefore study agent harness distillation: using a domain- or instance-optimized harness as training-time guidance and transferring the behaviors it induces into model weights, so that its gains survive under a single fixed target harness. The challenge is that the two harnesses differ in action space and available information, so guidance from the optimized harness cannot serve directly as supervision for the target one. We introduce Harness-Zero, which enables harness distillation through agent-as-harness. Guided by the optimized harness, a harnessing agent corrects student responses before execution in the target harness's action space, turning harness guidance into training demonstrations. Fine-tuning on the resulting trajectories internalizes harness-induced behavior into the model, so the specialized harness can be removed at deployment. Our experiments spanning knowledge work, tool use, and science domains show that: (1) For frontier LLMs using the same evolved harness, agent-as-harness outperforms code-as-harness. (2) With the specialized harness removed at deployment, Harness-Zero improves the base model's macro-average task success from 23.3% to 44.3%, even exceeding the 41.7% it reaches with that harness still attached, while reducing inference token usage by 27%-86%. (3) Harness-Zero recovers harness-induced behaviors absent from the base model, with 82.3% average recovery across 28 patterns in the three domains.

☆ Option-GRPO: Hierarchical RL Against AI Slop in Code Generation for Unseen SDKs EACL 2027
Prashank Kadam
Large language models increasingly produce AI slop in generated code: verbose, redundant, non-idiomatic implementations that pass functional checks but bloat codebases. The problem compounds on SDKs released after the model's training cutoff, where the model has neither idiomatic priors nor compositional understanding. We present Option-GRPO, a hierarchical reinforcement learning framework that adapts a code LLM to an unseen SDK from its source code alone, without human-written usage examples or test cases, and that explicitly optimizes for both functional correctness and code elegance. Our key insight is that SDK source code contains compositional structure that defines a hierarchy of reusable code patterns (code options) which simultaneously serve as the building blocks of a curriculum and the action space for efficient policy optimization. We show that code options induce a valid semi-Markov decision process, give conditional bounds under which shorter option description length implies more concise and, in expectation, more API-efficient programs, and analyze under stated assumptions how the option hierarchy improves credit assignment by a factor of $\sqrt{T/K}$. Using Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with execution-based rewards at each curriculum level, Option-GRPO discovers SDK idioms, composes them into patterns, and assembles patterns into complete programs. Experiments on 12 unseen SDKs with a 32B-parameter base model demonstrate 61.4% pass@1, outperforming flat GRPO by 8.7 points, ExploraCoder by 11.2 points, and ReCode by 6.3 points. Option-GRPO produces solutions that use 34.7% fewer API calls and are 27% shorter than flat-GRPO outputs while maintaining higher correctness, directly attacking the slop problem.

☆ FutureBench: A Renewable Framework for Evaluating Knowledge-Context Conflict in LLMs
Yi-Cheng Lai, Hen-Hsen Huang
Asking a model to check retrieved text can also tell it which source to trust. We introduce FutureBench, a renewable framework for evaluating knowledge-context conflict using timestamped questions, controlled passage triplets, and no-context probes. Across 3,629 questions and six models, Verify (a prompt combining checking with passage priority) raises false-answer adoption in all five models tested on pre-cutoff events, mainly by displacing answers that were correct under Baseline, which allows both passage evidence and model knowledge. On post-cutoff events, only o4-mini increases adoption (+13.7 percentage points). A four-prompt comparison on 902 post-cutoff questions finds that passage priority without checking raises adoption, while moving to Verify lowers it in all five models. Adversarial-Aware, which warns of misleading passages and prioritizes model knowledge, restores 82.5-98.9% of displaced pre-cutoff answers with under one point of change in true-passage accuracy. FutureBench supports repeated evaluation on fixed cohorts and extension to new events as models change.
☆ Safe to Omit, Worth Omitting? Keeping the Answer and Cutting Latency When an LLM Agent Skips Another's Message ICLR2027
Xinyang Li, Chih-Hsuan Yang, Xiaolong Ma, Anjir Ahmed Chowdhury, Vikram Vasudevan, Rajeev Thakur, Feng Yan
In multi-agent systems, a receiver agent often waits for a helper agent's message (text or its latent KV cache), which can improve performance but adds latency. We study when a receiver agent can skip the message safely (the final answer rarely changes) and beneficially (the time saved exceeds the cost of deciding). Our mechanism reads the receiver agent's confidence without the message and uses a finite-sample test to certify a threshold at which, with high probability, at most 5% of skipped answers change; if none passes, the system keeps communicating. We test it on OpenBookQA, ARC-Challenge, and MMLU-Pro with three Qwen model pairs, on cross-family agents, and on SQuAD and GSM8K. Four insights emerge. First, oracle headroom is weak evidence that messages help: control inputs without answer-specific content, matched to change answers as often as real messages, reproduce a median 76% of the oracle's gain. Second, whether a message can be skipped depends on how often and how predictably it alters the answer, not on its accuracy; every certified KV-cache path is less accurate than the receiver agent alone. Third, where text messages help, skipping keeps most of their gain: on unseen questions in the four such settings, the policies skip 59 to 80% and keep an estimated 76 to 95% of the gain. In two prospective tests with all rules fixed in advance, our mechanism skips a three-agent system's verifier on 39.9% of sealed questions, keeping 12.55 of its 12.80-point gain, and a 32B helper on 68.4% of held-out questions, keeping its whole gain. Fourth, safety does not determine efficiency: at batch size one, skipping text saves hundreds of milliseconds per query and skipping KV cache far less. Certification extends to SQuAD but not to GSM8K. In eleven tests on unseen questions, every certified policy we could test kept its change rate's 95% upper bound below 5% under our answer parsers.
comment: 58 pages, 11 figures, 41 tables. Code: https://github.com/Piedpiperrr/safe-to-omit; data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/AgentsSci/ICLR2027-SafeToOmit
☆ Language Models Make New Research Sound Old
Guy Zyskind
Researchers are increasingly using large language models (LLMs) to validate research questions. We find that LLMs make open research questions sound partly studied and overstate what related papers establish. Models recognize most of these overstatements when asked to check the papers' full texts separately. On 40 questions drawn from newly accepted papers, GPT-6-Astra correctly judges 98% to be unstudied, but states that status plainly in only 8% of ordinary answers. All six frontier systems show reporting gaps of 30-90 percentage points. Across 22 models given identical papers, higher capability is associated with more accurate judgments and larger reporting gaps. This reporting failure is asymmetric: models correctly identify studied questions in 88-100% of ordinary answers when we supply the answering papers. To correct this, we build a fixed answer from each model's judgment and separate checks of cited papers. This raises Astra's clear reporting of open status from 8% to 95%, with improvements in all six systems. Finally, we predict reviewers' novelty grades by combining several models' written ratings with a rule learned from graded examples, reaching the performance range reported by Think-Probe-Respond. Unlike TPR, our approach needs no access to model weights or internal activations, so it also works with closed-weight frontier models.
☆ Can LLMs Understand What They Predict? Evaluating Latent Causal Structure Inference in Financial Markets
Yixiang Zheng, Yuechen Jiang, Zhiwei Liu, Jimin Huang, Yuyan Wang, Sophia Ananiadou
Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly evaluated through their ability to predict future outcomes, implicitly assuming that predictive success reflects an understanding of the underlying system. However, accurate predictions can often be achieved without recovering the latent mechanisms that generate observable outcomes. To examine this assumption, we introduce LUCID, a benchmark that evaluates whether models can infer latent market structures from event descriptions and historical financial observations. Models generate event-induced causal graphs representing inferred market structure and are evaluated through a reconstruction-based framework. Our evaluation of 6 open-weight and proprietary LLMs shows that adding their generated graphs generally reduces reconstruction quality compared with using historical observations alone, although a few metrics improve. These findings suggest that inferring informative, event-specific market structures from observable evidence remains challenging for the evaluated LLMs.
☆ Dreaming Up Authors: Factual Prevalence and LLM Hallucinations in Academic Authorship Attribution EMNLP 2026
Sijia Zhong, Tamir Yirga, Yijia Ma, Thai Le, Yifan Hu
Large Language Models (LLMs) are widely used for question answering but are prone to hallucinations, yet the underlying causes, particularly the role of factual prevalence in training data, remain underexplored. In this work, we study this through academic authorship attribution, hypothesizing that hallucinations are more likely for less prevalent papers. Using citation count as a proxy for factual prevalence, we curate a dataset of 1.92 million papers across eight scientific disciplines with stratified samples by citation level. Experiments on three state-of-the-art LLMs confirm our hypothesis, revealing a robust inverse relationship between hallucination rate and citation count across all models and disciplines, with hallucination rates exceeding 98% for rarely cited papers and falling below 37% for highly cited ones. Our in-depth analysis further shows that these failures primarily reflect retrieval limitations rather than knowledge absence, and hidden-state probing of open-source models shows that factual prevalence is encoded in pre-generation representations, providing convergent mechanistic evidence for the prevalence-hallucination relationship. The code and data are available at https://github.com/quinnzhong1/dreaming-up-authors.

☆ From Arraignment to Resolution: Transformer Models of Court Cases EMNLP 2026
Kory Rosen, Aron Culotta
The criminal legal system is composed of many complex decision points, such as which charges are pursued, how bond is determined, plea deal negotiations, and sentencing. Given the profound impact of such decisions, there is a growing need to increase the transparency within this system. While prior machine learning approaches have focused on modeling single decisions (e.g., recidivism), this paper proposes a transformer-based time series model to forecast the outcome of the next proceeding in a case (e.g., charges accepted, bond set, plea deal made, etc.). By modeling a case as an event sequence, we enable a more nuanced analysis to support better oversight and understanding of decision making. We conduct an empirical analysis of thousands of cases using a newly constructed dataset derived from publicly available court records of a mid-sized U.S. city. We fit models that combine textual data from clerk notes with structured information about the defendant, prior charges, and courtroom. We demonstrate how the model can be used to provide insights into decision-making and help court observers identify emerging issues within the jurisdiction studied.

☆ GRAFT: Execution-Guided Genetic Recombination for Agent Design
Sevda Ebrahimi, Mohammed Saidul Islam, Xiaodan Zhu, Elham Dolatabadi
Automated agent design searches for executable architectures that improve LLM-based agent performance, with evolutionary methods using language-model crossover to recombine promising mechanisms for task decomposition, model coordination, and verification. This search, however, is typically guided by aggregate fitness alone, which hides the instance-level successes and failures behind each score. We introduce Execution Guided Genetic Recombination for Agent Design (GRAFT), which uses aggregate fitness for selection and survival and parent-specific execution evidence to guide semantic crossover with integrated mutation. Its feedback harness reuses bounded samples of successful and failed runs from fitness evaluation, withholding reference answers and requiring no additional task executions. We evaluate GRAFT across six reasoning and programming settings using open-weight optimizer and executor models. It achieves a mean test accuracy of 82.0%, exceeding the strongest baseline by 9.8% points. Controlled ablations show that adding execution evidence improves test accuracy with initialization, search rules, repair policies, and evaluation budgets held fixed. Our results support the value of parent-specific execution evidence for evolutionary agent design.
☆ BOS Summary: The First Token Matters
Hui Zhu, Saleh Zare Zade, Rafi Ibn Sultan, Deng Pan, Arun Ross, Dongxiao Zhu
Large vision-language models (LVLMs) and language models (LLMs) often exhibit attention sinks, whereby particular positions, such as the beginning-of-sequence (BOS) token, receive a disproportionate share of attention. Under standard causal masking, however, the BOS query attends only to itself, so the representation that later tokens repeatedly access carries no information about the input. We introduce BOS Summary, a training-free inference-time method that repurposes this persistent pathway to carry input context. At a single transformer layer during prefill, BOS Summary relaxes the causal constraint for the BOS query row alone, letting BOS aggregate all visual and textual input tokens into a self-derived context representation while every other token retains standard causal attention. The enriched representation then remains accessible to the model throughout decoding, with no additional parameters, auxiliary modules, or decoding-time overhead. Across multiple VLM and LLM families and eight multimodal and text-only benchmarks, BOS Summary improves over direct and Chain-of-Thought prompting and outperforms recent sink-intervention methods on multi-step reasoning tasks. A mechanistic analysis shows that making BOS input-specific also weakens its sink dominance and redistributes attention toward content-bearing prompt tokens, indicating that changing what a sink carries reshapes how later layers allocate attention.
☆ SpecLook: Speculating Where to Look for Fast and Reliable Agentic MLLMs
Xi Ding, Jiawei Zhang
Agentic multimodal large language models (MLLMs) that think with images interleave reasoning with zoom-in tool calls. However, this sequential loop is slow, and its answers carry no reliability score. Prior speculation of tokens, reasoning steps, or answers keeps the loop, and answer speculation also accepts confident errors. We find that the loop mostly adds one zoom-in step, with which a small model is ahead of the agent on most splits. Our key insight is to speculate the agent's next observation, which, unlike a drafted answer, can be verified, corrected, and used to test the answer. We propose SpecLook, a training-free speculate-and-verify framework with three levels. A small model drafts a glimpse and re-answers from it, and the question ends if the answer is unchanged and confident. Otherwise, the agent's large model verifies and corrects the glimpse without tools, and their answers are fused. Only questions that still lack evidence reach the agent, in one forward pass that SpecLook-Fast skips, so no question runs the loop. The trace scores every answer's reliability without labels. Extensive experiments on V*, HR-Bench, and POPE show that both are more accurate than every baseline on every split. SpecLook reaches 87.2% and 86.2% mean accuracy with 7.7x and 4.7x speedups over the DeepEyes-7B and Thyme-RL agents, and SpecLook-Fast reaches 9.9x and 6.3x.

☆ Formal Adequacy Index (based on Polish-language material)
Paweł Patreucha
Linguistic formality is one of the dimensions of stylistic variation in speech; however, simply measuring its level does not allow us to determine whether the chosen mode of communication is appropriate in a given situation. The aim of this article is to present the concept of a formal adequacy index (based on Polish-language material), which enables a quantitative assessment of how well the formality of a text matches the expectations arising from the communicative context. The proposed approach is based on the separate modelling of observed formality, determined on the basis of the linguistic features of the text, and expected formality, determined on the basis of the characteristics of the communicative situation. Adequacy is assessed by comparing these two measures, taking into account contextual tolerance thresholds, which may differ for a lack of formality and an excess of formality. The potential applications of the index are illustrated by an analysis of Polish-language emails containing apologies for being late, written by human language users or generated by a large language model. The implementation presented here is preliminary in nature and requires further calibration and empirical validation. The main contribution of this article is the proposal of a general methodological framework for the quantitative study of formal adequacy in various communicative contexts.

☆ Tracking Representations in LLM Reasoning Traces for Iterated Games
Chaipat Tirapongprasert, Rajarshi Das, Parijat Dube
Large language model (LLM) agents achieve state-of-the-art performance across domains through chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning. Despite their ubiquitous use, little is understood about the relationship between the externalized trace and the model's actual computation. We address this by investigating how LLM agents make decisions in iterative games with incomplete information. Using a series of mechanistic interpretability techniques, we find that the model forms and iteratively revises an internal representation of the game throughout the CoT. Once the model prematurely commits to an incorrect belief, it rarely recovers. This failure is manifested in both the literal trace and the layer activations. Ultimately, as multi-agent systems are more prevalent, our work opens the door to a more focused diagnosis of their strategic reasoning errors. For illustrative purposes, we offer an interactive visualizer of our methodology on selected sample traces at https://interp-self.github.io/gametrace/

☆ QDA: Closed-Loop LLM Quantization Design with Agents
Xingyang Li, Tianlang Zhao, Song Han, Ligeng Zhu
Post-training quantization is the standard way to serve large language models at low cost. However, the best quantization recipe differs across models and precisions. As a result, experts must redesign it for every new model, which is time-consuming and often sub-optimal. Fortunately, an LLM agent can take over this design by refining the recipe over rounds of building and evaluating candidates, so that more rounds lead to better recipes. However, evaluating one quantized reasoning model takes tens of GPU-hours, and combining methods from different codebases means porting each to the new architecture by hand. We present QDA, a system in which an agent designs the recipe for each model and precision. To make evaluation fast, QDA scores each candidate in minutes by its output KL divergence from the original model on reasoning traces that the agent never sees, and checks promising ones with long generations to catch failures such as repetition loops. To make building fast, QDA provides a library of public PTQ methods that are compatible with one another and with new model architectures. Across four reasoning models, QDA improves accuracy over the strongest public recipe by 0.9, 1.4 and 2.2 percentage points on average at W4A16, W4A8 and W3A16, using 64 GPU-hours per search. On the 320B-parameter MoE model GLM-5.3-Flash at NVFP4 W4A4, it recovers 98.3% of the FP8 model's accuracy, against 96.9% for the best vendor checkpoint. Every QDA checkpoint also runs on existing serving kernels with no inference overhead. We will release the library, the evaluator and the harness.

☆ Chorus: Shared N-gram Embeddings with Layer-wise Adaptation
Stephen Chung, Wenyu Du
Language models can gain capacity at little computational cost from n-gram embedding tables: large tables of learned vectors for short sequences of consecutive tokens, which are looked up and added to the model's input or to its intermediate layers. Recent designs commonly use a separate n-gram embedding table for each layer, which makes the tables costly in memory and prevents layers from sharing what they learn. Sharing a single table, however, gives every layer the same embedding and leaves no room for layer-wise preferences, and it performs poorly in practice. We propose Chorus, in which one set of n-gram embedding tables serves the whole network and every layer reads it in its own way. Each layer maps the refined embedding through its own projection layer, after rescaling its components by factors computed from its current hidden state. Chorus matches the quality of existing n-gram methods with 87% smaller tables: with 1.2B table parameters it predicts text better than FactorEngram and DeepSeek's Engram do with 9.6B. It outperforms Engram, TN-gram and FactorEngram in every setting where we compared them, across table sizes from 20M to 9.6B parameters. We also show that the recently proposed memory-efficient optimizer Ember works well with Chorus, saving 99.2% of table-optimizer memory compared with AdamW without degrading training performance. These results enable further scaling of n-gram embedding tables along the table-size axis by reducing memory use and allowing shared knowledge to be reused across layers.
☆ Ambient LLMs: Data-Efficient Supervised Fine-tuning via Temperature Matching
Giannis Daras, Marios Papachristou, Adam Klivans, Antonio Torralba, Constantinos Daskalakis
Collecting large-scale, diverse, and high-quality post-training datasets for autoregressive language models is challenging. Consequently, Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) is typically applied to datasets significantly smaller than those used for pre-training, which leads to overfitting and the catastrophic forgetting of the tails of the data distribution. To prevent this collapse, we introduce Ambient SFT, a principled framework for leveraging imperfect predictions to augment the fine-tuning set. In our method, a "frozen" version of the pre-trained model serves as a noisy teacher that the fine-tuned model must match at a coarse level. In particular, we require that the high-temperature versions of the conditional distributions produced by the post-training model should match the high-temperature versions of the pre-training ones. The temperature hyperparameter controls the level of trust in the pre-trained model, with the limit $T\to \infty$ reverting back to regular SFT and $T=1$ to knowledge distillation. We demonstrate that our approach outperforms standard SFT across domain adaptation, instruction-tuning, and reasoning, leading for up to $21\%$ average relative improvement to standard SFT.

☆ What Does a Bias Head Measure? Non-Identifiability in Head-Level Bias Attribution ACL
S. M Seefat Alam
Methods that localise social bias in large language models to individual attention heads and remove it by ablation are validated by their effect on a fairness metric, never by evidence that the statistic selecting the heads measures bias. We audit this on Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct and Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct. The head map correlates at $ρ= -0.950$ and $-0.768$ with a label-blind response-length map, apparently identifying a surface confound. An intervention that breaks the length-label coupling rejects this reading: with the prompt held fixed, response length explains 1% of the statistic's variance. What does explain it is prompt identity, at 64% of variance, in an ordering that is not monotonic in length. Within any fixed prompt the fairness label, response length and the presence of a protected-group token are collinear at $φ= 0.95$, partly by definition of the labelling rule, so no observational analysis can attribute the map to any of them, and no method in this family performs an experiment that could. The substantive claims fail independently: bias selectivity is not late-layer-concentrated on either model under either reference reading, and no head set we tested reduces the unfairness rate when ablated. Its complement, scored on the opposite label, reliably destroys refusal behaviour ($p = 2.7\times10^{-5}$), demonstrating that the pipeline detects effects at this scale.

☆ Structure--Residual Inductive Biases for Language Model Pretraining
Hai Huang, Bartosz Żuk, Rassul Magauin, Jiaxin Huang, Xue Liu
Next-token prediction is relied upon to learn both predictive behavior and internal representations. We ask whether representation structure itself should be an explicit inductive bias. Motivated by the two-part coding view of Kolmogorov complexity, we study a structure--residual principle in which a representation separates structure from realization detail. We instantiate this principle in two complementary ways. Boundary Bottleneck Concept (BBC) routes information from token segments through boundary carriers while preserving local autoregressive computation. Continuous Concept (CC) regularizes representation geometry and induces a spectral decomposition into low-frequency structure and high-frequency residual. We derive coding bounds for both views and show that multiplicity-corrected boundary attention becomes exact as the within-segment path energy vanishes. Empirically, BBC and CC exhibit strikingly different depth preferences: BBC works best in early layers, whereas CC is most effective in later layers. At 300M training tokens, combining BBC and CC improves NLL by 1.44\% on GPT-2 Medium. These gains persist with training runs up to 20B tokens. Representation diagnostics show that BBC increases the relative predictive importance of boundary states, while CC reduces spectral residual energy. Together, these results suggest that structure--residual biases can improve language-model pretraining beyond next-token prediction. Code: https://github.com/haihuang-ml/structure-residual

Nhi Hoai Doan, Tatsuya Hiraoka, Munachiso Samuel Nwadike, Kentaro Inui

☆ TextSphere: Efficient Text-to-Image Generation via Shared Hyperspherical Alignment and Uniformity
Yixia Zhang, Girish A. Koushik, Xilu Wang, Wenwu Wang
Recent advances in text-to-image generation have substantially improved image quality, particularly through diffusion-based methods that require multiple inference steps. In contrast, Sphere Encoder offers an efficient generative framework capable of producing images with fewer forward steps. However, extending it to free-form text is nontrivial, because its class-conditioned design lacks cross-modal alignment. To address this challenge, we propose TextSphere, an efficient text-to-image generative framework that extends Sphere Encoder with language conditioning and cross-modal alignment. We incorporate pretrained CLIP text embeddings through adaptive layer normalization (AdaLN) to guide image generation while retaining the original spherical sampling mechanism. To bridge the cross-modal representation gap, we introduce a Spherical Semantic Alignment and Uniformity (SSAU) objective that jointly promotes semantic alignment and representation uniformity on the hypersphere. Experiments on CUB-200-2011, MS-COCO-2014, and Flickr30k demonstrate that TextSphere consistently reduces generation FID (gFID) over the text-conditioned baseline while achieving reconstruction FID (rFID) below 1.0. With only 4 and 6 sampling steps, TextSphere achieves lower gFID than the compared diffusion- and GAN-based models trained from scratch on CUB-200-2011 and MS-COCO-2014, respectively. We further observe that improved reconstruction fidelity does not necessarily yield better generation quality. In addition, we use Partial Least Squares Singular Value Decomposition (PLS-SVD) to analyze cross-modal alignment and modality gap, offering insight into the effectiveness of our approach.

☆ Mind the Gap: A Deployed Multi-Agent System for Documentation Maintenance AAAI 2027
Lorenzo Masoero
The "AI revolution" has enabled teams across the technology industry to deliver software features at unprecedented velocity. While code evolves faster than ever, customer-facing documentation often struggles to stay up to speed. As users increasingly rely on LLM-based agents that consume documentation as their primary interface, the impact of this documentation gap worsens: stale information propagates and is treated as authoritative. Signals for identifying where documentation has fallen behind already exist in abundance, both explicit (e.g., resolved support tickets revealing what documentation lacks) and implicit (e.g., code-documentation discrepancies detectable from automated loops). We deploy a multi-agent system, built around a triage agent we call "TriAgent," that continuously extracts these signals and repairs documentation drift. Over 43 days of shadow-mode operation, our system triaged 499 tickets and surfaced 92 gaps in the documentation. Using a panel of LLM judges from independent model families, 81.1% of TriAgent's verifiable assertions were correct (245 of the 302 assertions the human resolution addresses; majority of three judges); on length-comparable pairs the panel preferred TriAgent's response in 70.5% of pairs and the human engineer's in 23.2% (the rest had no judge majority). Importantly, agent- and human-sourced replies tended to be complementary, with the human often surfacing insights that were either buried within the existing documentation or missing from it entirely; our system turns each such insight into a documentation gap and, after human review, a fix. Repairing a gap measurably improved the agent's later answer: when the agent re-answered the same ticket after the repair, blinded judges preferred the post-repair answer over its own pre-repair answer 71.4% of the time (n=35). A blinded human audit confirms strong alignment on self-improvement.

☆ The Illusion of Grounding: Do Vision-Language Models Weigh Retrieved Text and Images Like an Ideal Observer?
Basavaraj R M, Harshal Chandel
Retrieval-augmented vision-language models (VLMs) answer from two sources of evidence that can each fail: the retrieved text can be wrong or noisy, and the image can be degraded or contradicted. We introduce GIB-200K, 200,000 image-question items built from COCO and Visual Genome, each expanded into controlled stress conditions: hard-negative retrieval noise (1-20 documents), visual ambiguity (Gaussian and motion blur, low-resource crops) and cross-modal conflict with counterfactual flip pairs. Instead of only measuring degradation, we ask how models combine the two modalities. From ideal Bayesian cue combination we derive a testable prediction that yields formal definitions of Knowledge Inertia (text over-weighted relative to the model's own image evidence) and Sensory Capture (the reverse), and we estimate the weights with a measurement-error-corrected regression whose recovery of known weights we verify. A counterfactual flip decomposition separates modality bias from concept priors, and a Grounding Illusion Score measures how much clean accuracy is not grounded in the image. We evaluate 4 open models on one frozen, seeded evaluation split of 2,000 GIB-200K items. All models are accurate on clean inputs (92.5%-94.2%) but adverse accuracy falls to 41.3%-59.9%. The two LLaVA models over-weight the text (Knowledge Inertia, w_T/w_I = 1.229 and 1.322), whereas InternVL3 favours the image (w_T/w_I = 0.680); between 34.8% and 71.4% of the clean answers that are correct are abandoned when the document lies. A training-free consistency check that compares the answer with and without retrieval trades coverage for accuracy under misleading documents, while falling back to the image fails when the image is heavily degraded. All per-instance outputs, code, and an audit that recomputes every reported open-model number are released.

☆ Dynamic Task Allocation with Dialogue IROS 2026
Matthew Groholski, Weiwei Gu, Nakul Gopalan
Effective multi-agent collaboration requires agents to adapt task allocation as goals and responsibilities evolve. Prior task-allocation frameworks often assume static goals, full observability, or rely on large language models (LLMs) without explicit, verifiable beliefs about task allocation or intent. These assumptions limit their applicability to real-world collaboration, where agents often lack full information about each other's actions, progress, or local environment. To support dynamic task allocation under partial observability, we introduce a communication-augmented Bayesian task-allocation framework. These results indicate that our agent is more efficient in dynamic task allocation than baselines in partially observable environments, and that communication is important as available information decreases.

☆ Nunchi: Ears Help Eyes in Multi-Party Conversations
JunYeong Park, Shijing Wang, Seonwook Park, Michael Stengel, Shalini De Mello, Sang-Il Choi, Hyung Jin Chang
Identifying whom a person looks at is central to understanding multi-party conversations, yet gaze geometry alone becomes ambiguous as more people appear. Conversations offer cues that geometry lacks, such as who is speaking and what is said, but no gaze dataset provides them for many multi-party scenes. We introduce SORBET, a benchmark of 3,340 samples from 1,578 clips with synchronized speech, transcripts, and attended person labels, covering more multi-party scenes than existing gaze datasets. Prior gaze models struggle to use these cues, which come in different forms, must be linked to the right person, and can conflict with gaze geometry. We propose Nunchi, a framework that constructs a text prompt from the transcript and the outputs of gaze and speaker models, links every cue to the candidates with shared labels, and lets a vision-language model (VLM) weigh gaze geometry against conversational cues to identify the attended person. Nunchi outperforms the strongest existing method by 4.8 percentage points. On SORBET, conversational cues help whereas additional visual cues do not, and the gains hold across three VLMs, with automatic transcripts, and on two existing datasets.

☆ Large-scale Neuron Community Discovery Reveals Neuron-Sparse Behaviors in Language Models
Igor Shilov, Thomas Fel, Tal Haklay, Lucius Bushnaq, Owen Lewis
What reusable units of computation can we discover within a language model's neurons? Prior work has shown that individual forward passes can often be sparsified post-hoc to small groups of neurons, but it has been unclear whether these groups form coherent computational entities reused across inputs. We introduce neuron community discovery, an unsupervised method for finding neuron groups that recur across prompts and are causally necessary and sufficient, within the layers they span, for common computations. The method combines gradient-based attribution, a graph connecting neurons that are repeatedly attributed together, and community detection. Applied to Llama-3.1-8B using ten million prompts, it discovers thousands of candidate communities. We evaluate them on held-out prompts through three causal tests: community necessity, sufficiency within a layer subset, and layer necessity. These tests identify groups whose removal disrupts predictions and whose retention preserves predictions despite ablating the remaining MLP neurons in the tested layers. Discovered communities support functions like grammatical agreement, token-probability calibration, fixed phrases, in-context copying, addition, and multiplication. With communities capped at 128 neurons across all layers, 10.3% of held-out prompts have at least one layer's contribution explained on a neuron-sparse basis. Coverage remains 8.7% when requiring at least four layers and 3.3% when requiring at least twelve. These results show that small groups of existing neurons can provide reusable computational components across prompts, and that such groups can be discovered at scale.
☆ Natural Language Programming: Rethinking Language, Memory, and Cognition in the Era of Generative AI
Giovanni Adorni, Daniele Grosso
Generative Artificial Intelligence is transforming the role of natural language in computational systems. In classical Natural Language Processing (NLP), language was treated as an object of computation: something to analyse, classify, translate, or generate. Large Language Models (LLMs) challenge this perspective, enabling natural language to function increasingly as an executable cognitive medium through which humans structure reasoning, orchestrate problem solving, and regulate hybrid human/AI workflows. This paper introduces Natural Language Programming (NLPg) as a theoretical and epistemological extension of traditional NLP. Rather than focusing on language understanding alone, NLPg conceptualises prompts as dynamic cognitive artefacts that externalise goals, constraints, assumptions, validation criteria, and intermediate reasoning states. Prompting is thus not merely a technical interaction technique, but a form of dialogic cognitive programming operating within distributed human/AI memory architectures. Building on theories of distributed cognition, extended mind, metacognition, and dialogic learning, the paper proposes the notion of Dialogic Working Memory, describing how prompts and conversational states function as temporary external memory structures supporting reasoning continuity and reflective regulation. We further map existing memory architectures (BTMA, SKGE, RMR) onto this framework, illustrating how episodic, semantic, and metacognitive layers support long-horizon conversational coherence. Finally, implications for computational linguistics and AI/mediated knowledge systems are discussed, suggesting a post symbolic and post interface paradigm in which natural language operates simultaneously as communication medium, executable specification, cognitive scaffold, and distributed memory architecture.

♻ ☆ Learning User Simulators with Turing Rewards
Yingshan Susan Wang, Cedegao E. Zhang, Linlu Qiu, Zexue He, Pengyuan Li, Alex Pentland, Roger P. Levy, Yoon Kim
Learning to simulate human users in interactive settings could advance the training of agent assistants, evaluation of personalization systems, research in the social sciences, and more. Existing approaches generally do so by training a large language model (LLM) to match a single ground truth response, either by maximizing the log probability or by using a similarity reward. We instead propose Turing-RL: a Turing-Test-based reinforcement learning approach for training user simulator models. Turing-RL uses a discriminative Turing reward with an LLM judge to score how indistinguishable a generated response is from the real user's given the user's history, and the user simulator LLM learns to produce responses indistinguishable from what the user could have said with such rewards. Across two different domains--conversational chat and Reddit forum discussion--we find that Turing-RL consistently outperforms baseline methods on both LLM and human evaluation metrics. Our study suggests that optimizing for indistinguishability, rather than response matching, is effective for learning user simulators.
♻ ☆ HorizonBench: Long-Horizon Personalization with Evolving Preferences
Shuyue Stella Li, Bhargavi Paranjape, Kerem Oktar, Zhongyao Ma, Gelin Zhou, Lin Guan, Na Zhang, Sem Park, Lin Chen, Diyi Yang, Yulia Tsvetkov, Asli Celikyilmaz
Personal assistants now talk with the same user for months, and users' preferences change as their lives change; an assistant that keeps serving a preference the user has outgrown fails that user. Real conversations do not record when or why a preference changed, and existing benchmarks either hold preferences fixed or have the user state the change, so they cannot tell whether a model forgot what the user said or failed to update its belief about the user. We introduce HorizonBench and its data generator, which samples how a user's preferences change and generates six months of conversation from that sequence; each change follows a life event that the user mentions without stating the new preference. A human longitudinal preference dataset that we collect and release, 83,024 ratings from 160 people over 13 weeks, sets how often and how much life events change preferences. HorizonBench contains 4,245 questions from 360 users, and when a preference has changed, its earlier value is one of the answer options. All 25 frontier models we evaluate choose the outdated preference more often than evenly spread errors would predict, and the best reaches 52.8% accuracy. Two of the newest models score no higher when given only the relevant conversations than with the full history, yet one added sentence stating the change raises their accuracy by 11 to 22 points, and retrieval and memory methods do not recover the lost accuracy. Remembering what a user said and knowing what the user prefers now are therefore separate capabilities, and HorizonBench, its generator, and the human dataset support research on both.
comment: 26 pages, 6 figures, 19 tables
♻ ☆ Disentangling generalization and memorization in large language models using chess
Leonard S. Pleiss, Maximilian Schiffer, Robert K. von Weizsaecker
Large Language Models (LLMs) exhibit remarkable capabilities, yet it remains unclear to what extent these reflect sophisticated recall or genuine reasoning ability. We introduce chess as a controlled testbed aimed at disentangling these faculties. Leveraging the game's structure and scalable engine evaluations, we construct a taxonomy of positions varying in density of relevant priors -- ranging from common states solvable by memorization to completely novel ones requiring generalization. Crucially, our approach achieves this distinction without requiring explicit knowledge of the models' training data. Applying this taxonomy, we combine a longitudinal analysis of the GPT lineage with a rigorous evaluation of contemporary models, including Claude Opus and Gemini. Our analysis reveals a steep gradient: performance consistently degrades as the density of relevant priors decreases. Notably, for tasks with few relevant priors, base model performance regresses to the random-play baseline, and all models propose illegal moves at sharply increasing rates, indicating that they fail to reliably apply the fixed rules of the game in unfamiliar positions. While newer models improve, progress slows significantly for tasks with sparse priors. Furthermore, while reasoning-augmented inference improves performance, its relative marginal benefit per token decreases in the absence of relevant priors. These results suggest limitations in systematic generalization, highlighting the need for mechanisms beyond scale to achieve robust performance when deprived of relevant priors.
♻ ☆ Sparse Layers are Critical to Scaling Looped Language Models
Ryan Lee, Jacob Biloki, Edward J. Hu, Jonathan May
Looped language models repeat a set of transformer layers through depth, reducing memory costs and providing natural early-exit points at loop boundaries. However, looped models do not scale as favorably as standard transformers with unique layers. We compare standard and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) transformers, with and without looping, and find two main results. First, we find Looped-MoE models scale better than the standard baseline while dense looped models do not. We trace this to routing divergence between loops: in Looped-MoE models, different experts are activated on each pass through the same shared layers, recovering expressivity without additional parameters. Our second finding is that looped models have better compute-quality trade-offs with early exits than standard models. Because each loop ends with the same layers that produce the final output, loop boundaries are superior exit points, as confirmed by earlier output convergence at these points. In sum, we provide a clear direction for scaling looped models: a Looped-MoE model with early exits can not only beat standard transformers at scale, but also enable significant memory and inference savings with minimal degradation in quality.
♻ ☆ Colosseum: Auditing Collusion in Cooperative Multi-Agent Systems
Mason Nakamura, Abhinav Kumar, Saswat Das, Sahar Abdelnabi, Saaduddin Mahmud, Ferdinando Fioretto, Shlomo Zilberstein, Eugene Bagdasarian
Multi-agent systems, where LLM agents communicate through free-form language, enable sophisticated coordination for solving complex cooperative tasks. This surfaces a unique safety problem when a group of agents forms a coalition and colludes to pursue secondary goals and degrade the joint objective. In this paper, we present Colosseum, a framework for auditing LLM agents' collusive behavior in multi-agent settings. We ground how agents cooperate through a formal multi-agent decision-making framework and measure action-based collusive behavior in actions via regret relative to the cooperative optimum and compare it with communication-based collusive behavior. Colosseum enables audits of LLM agents for collusion under benign settings, different coalition objectives, persuasion tactics, and network topologies. We then introduce a new behavioral probe by creating secret communication channels between agents, showing that most out-of-the-box models exhibit a propensity to collude under this probe, which we term emergent collusion. Furthermore, we discover ``collusion on paper'' when agents plan to collude in text but often pick non-collusive actions. Colosseum provides a new way to audit collusion in cooperative multi-agent systems while presenting observations about how collusion emerges, what affects collusion efficacy, and which strategies may mitigate it.

♻ ☆ Swiss-Knife: A Framework for Reconfigurable Externalised Multi-Objective Alignment at Decode Time
Agnibh Karmakar, Mayur Parvatikar, Shreyash Dhoot, Amit Dhanda, Aman Chadha, Kapil Wanaskar, Vinija Jain, Amitava Das
Decode-time alignment methods steer a frozen language model by scoring candidate continuations with an external reward and selecting the maximiser. We argue that this shared design is a single degenerate point in a much larger space. We introduce Swiss-Knife, a framework for externalised multi-objective alignment in which the alignment specification is a first-class runtime object: hot-swappable scoring blades, a batch normaliser, a pairwise aggregation operator, and a selection rule. Six axioms characterise the admissible aggregation operators, and we prove a representation theorem: every operator satisfying them has the form $R_i = \sum_{j \neq i} g((μ_i - μ_j)/s(σ_i,σ_j))$, a two-parameter family containing probit and logistic comparison rules and pointwise argmax as named coordinates. Within it, pairwise aggregation is Lipschitz-stable under adversarial reward contamination while argmax is not, and Candidate-Batch Normalization (CBN) makes the weight simplex invariant to the rescalings under which reward models are only ever identified. Our reference instantiation pairs DPO-LoRA blades with an uncertainty-aware pairwise tournament. Sweeping the helpfulness/honesty/harmlessness simplex, it attains the best balanced frontier of six decode-time methods (harmonic $F_1$ 0.797 vs. 0.750 for the strongest baseline, $p < 10^{-14}$) with the lowest refusal rate and highest helpfulness of any arm, and reconfigures its objectives in 0.05 ms with no gradient computation. Ablating CBN costs 0.217 $F_1$, and the metadata confirms the predicted mechanism: the lowest-variance blade retains 9% of its nominal 33% influence, and the collapse follows the predicted ordering.

♻ ☆ Distinguishing Extraction Failures and Clinical Discordance with LLM Uncertainty on Radiology Reports ML4H 2026
Jerjes Aguirre-Chavez, Shant Malkasian, Roshun Sankaran, Brendan Crabb, Albert Hsiao
Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to structure data from unstructured text, but extraction errors remain inevitable. We evaluated whether LLM uncertainty could distinguish extraction failure from true clinical discordance. In 5{,}225 vessels from 1{,}045 patients undergoing coronary CT angiography (CCTA) for suspected coronary artery disease (CAD), we evaluated token-level and self-reported LLM uncertainty during extraction of vessel-level stenosis severity. Extraction failure was defined as disagreement between LLM-extracted stenosis and expert annotations. Clinical discordance was defined as disagreement in obstructive CAD status between CCTA and subsequent invasive coronary angiography (ICA). Incremental discrimination was assessed after adjustment for stenosis severity, calcium burden, and coronary vessel. Extraction failure was evaluated in 1{,}077 vessels from 253 patients with expert annotations, and clinical discordance in 1{,}641 vessels from 729 patients undergoing ICA within 90 days. Token-level Stenosis Margin showed the strongest association with extraction failure (adjusted OR, 4.15 [95\% CI, 2.57--6.69]; $P<0.001$) and clinical discordance (adjusted OR, 1.22 [95\% CI, 1.04--1.43]; $P=0.118$). Stenosis Margin significantly improved discrimination of extraction failure ($Δ$AUC, +0.06 [95\% CI, +0.03 to +0.09]; $P=0.001$), but not clinical discordance ($Δ$AUC, +0.01 [95\% CI, $-0.00$ to +0.02]; $P=0.245$). In a nested cohort of 138 vessels from 57 patients, 38 of 42 clinically discordant vessels (90\%) were extracted correctly. LLM uncertainty improved identification of extraction failure but not clinical discordance, suggesting that uncertainty may identify errors introduced during data extraction but may not generalize to discordance in underlying clinical data.

♻ ☆ XMerge: Cross-Axis Selection and Reconstructive Layer Merging for LLM Depth Compression NeurIPS 2026
Jundong Hu, Shekar Ramachandran
Removing complete transformer layers preserves a standard serving architecture, but existing depth-compression methods can lose substantial quality, and the loss varies unpredictably across models. We introduce XMerge, a post-training method with two components. Cross-axis selection identifies a block with low relative-magnitude and angular hidden-state change, and local boundary reconstruction re-fits the adjacent surviving block to match the original two-block output. XMerge uses no task labels or end-to-end fine-tuning, and it introduces neither architectural changes nor additional inference-time parameters. Across seven Llama and Qwen backbones (0.5B-8B), five published baselines, and three layer-reduction levels, its advantage over baselines is largest at the most aggressive removal: at k=4 it ranks first on six of seven backbones on CORE (a 22-task aggregate) and, separately, on six of seven on MMLU (five of seven on both at once), while avoiding the large perplexity increases of several competing operators. In a task-level bootstrap, the 95% confidence intervals for the three largest CORE margins exclude zero; the remaining margins are consistent with ties. Across the 14 (model, regime) cells it is also the only evaluated operator that never collapses, ranking top-2 in both zero-shot and in-context regimes; on a first calibration probe (one backbone) it is the best-calibrated operator. Ablations show that local reconstruction provides most of the gain, while cross-axis fusion helps when the two selection axes disagree. The additional construction cost is recovered through per-token decode savings after roughly tens of thousands of requests.

♻ ☆ MedExAgent: Training LLM Agents to Ask, Examine, and Diagnose in Noisy Clinical Environments
Yicheng Gao, Xiaolin Zhou, Yahan Li, Yue Zhao, Ruishan Liu
Real-world clinical diagnosis is a complex process in which the doctor is required to obtain information from both interaction with the patient and conducting medical exams. Additionally, the doctor needs to adapt to different patient personas, as well as noisy and incomplete information that can happen at any time during the process. However, existing benchmarks for medical LLMs and methods for automatic diagnosis largely simplify this process by reducing it to single-turn question answering, noise-free conversations, or sequential exam making, etc., ignoring the interactive and uncertain nature of clinical diagnosis. In this paper, we aim to address this gap by formalizing clinical diagnosis as a Partially Observable Markov Decision Process (POMDP) with three action types: questioning the patient, ordering medical exams as tool calls, and issuing a diagnosis. We also introduce a systematic noise model comprising seven patient noise types and three exam noise types. Using our proposed environment, we train an effective diagnosis agent, \textbf{MedExAgent}, through a two-stage pipeline that first performs supervised finetuning on synthetic conversations structured after the Calgary-Cambridge model for clinical interviews, and then applies DAPO to optimize a composite reward capturing diagnostic accuracy, tool call quality, and exam cost including financial cost and patient discomfort. Through extensive experiments and ablation studies, we demonstrate that MedExAgent achieves diagnostic performance comparable to larger models while maintaining cost-efficient examination strategies.
♻ ☆ Typed Decision Models under Prevalence Shift and Label Budgets: Decision Training, Calibration, and Implicit Priors
Abdul Rahman Iqbal, Muhammad Fawad Nasim, Amir Saleem
Typed decision models (TDMs) return probabilities over caller-defined options, and Jev's vendor describes it as calibrated. We ask whether vendor decision training improves a model over its backbone, whether a TDM's calibration and thresholds withstand prevalence (label) shift like a fine-tuned classifier's, and how many labels a trained model needs to overtake a TDM. We compare three TDMs (Jev, Tev1-4B, Nimble-9B), their base models and trained classifiers on identical candidate-set decisions from five public datasets (on intent datasets, within the gold label's intent group) under simulated shift, with 35 paired tests frozen before the confirmatory analysis (earlier test-data checks disclosed), and estimate label-budget curves. First, decision training is not always beneficial: Nimble-9B, which differs from Qwen3.5-9B only by a LoRA adapter, is better calibrated on intent tasks but 12 Macro-F1 points worse on financial sentiment, a loss that persists across prompt formats; Tev1-4B's largest gain over its backbone is a prompt-format effect. Second, on 50 fixed moderate shifts, Jev's top-label calibration error and threshold risk grow significantly more than a fine-tuned DeBERTa's only on financial sentiment, sensitive to the prior set; averaged over 2,000 shifts the gap is at most one point. Third, label-free EM correction with the model's implicit prior lowers Jev's shifted calibration error where it is highest, but labelled recalibration lowers it far more. Fourth, DeBERTa needs 100-250 labels to overtake Jev on hate speech and does not clearly overtake it on CLINC150 at any budget; a QLoRA adapter on Qwen3.5-4B overtakes Jev with at most 2,500 labels on every dataset, on CLINC150 only marginally and not under in-budget selection. The TDMs tested are neither uniformly better than their backbones nor uniquely fragile under shift; their lead over trained models depends on the task.

♻ ☆ Measuring and Attributing Evidence Sensitivity in Medical Vision-Language Models via Symmetric Counterfactual Controls
Bhaskar Gurram
Medical vision-language models (VLMs) asked closed clinical questions ("Is there a polyp?") can score well by relying on the question rather than the image, and current hallucination benchmarks cannot separate true visual discrimination from editing shortcuts or shifts in the answer threshold. We introduce a Counterfactual Evidence Framework that pairs each lesion erasure with a shape- and tool-matched control edit elsewhere in the image, adds same-site ring controls, restored and partial erasures, degraded views and natural negatives, tests on held-out inpainting tools, separates discrimination (the Evidence Sensitivity Gap, ESG, and AUROC) from the answer threshold, and checks the labels with blinded physician readers and hidden-state probes. Across 507 main-test cases (1,115 views), nine frontier VLMs claim 41.1% to 61.6% of erased lesions (ESG 0.269 to 0.470), whereas MedScope-4B, a Qwen3.5-4B agent trained on automatically built counterfactual trajectories, claims 9.6% (ESG 0.758; p <= 0.001 in all 27 primary comparisons). Physician readers support automated erasure in colonoscopy, where frontier models still claim 45% of erased lesions against 1% for MedScope-4B, but call a finding present on 54.7% of pooled erased reads across all sources, which bounds what unverified erasure can certify. Across 12 pre-registered training or system arms and 270 decision policies screened under a rule fixed in advance, every apparent utility gain comes from shifting the threshold rather than improving held-out discrimination, and probes point to perception as the remaining limit. We also report a 0/48 pneumothorax failure on an external cohort; retraining with pneumothorax patients in every stage finds 53% of pneumothoraxes on new patients without changing the main-test results.

♻ ☆ Localized LoRA-MoE: Mixture of Low-Rank Experts With Adaptive Routing
Babak Barazandeh, Subhabrata Majumdar, Vinay Prithyani, George Michailidis
Parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) methods such as LoRA adapt large models through a single low-rank update. Such updates are susceptible to gradient interference: when a training stream interleaves multiple operational contexts, conflicting updates to shared adapter weights collapse the adapter into an unspecialized average. While recent methods introduce block-wise isolation to remedy this issue, their topologies are static and cannot adapt to context switching or localized sensor degradation. In this work, we introduce Localized LoRA-MoE, a framework that combines localized spatial blocking with context-conditioned expert routing. We propose and evaluate two architectural variants: Block-Wise LoRA-MoE (centralized routing), which modulates the entire structural grid through a single context-conditioned gate, and Cell-Wise LoRA-MoE (decentralized routing), which assigns an independent gate to every coordinate cell of the matrix grid. Across three structured transformation benchmarks--a high-dimensional SVD matrix simulation, context-switched transformations over real tabular data, and spatial reconstruction under simulated sensor occlusion--both variants outperform static baselines under comparable adapter budgets. Both variants perform similarly when context shifts the grid uniformly, while cell-wise routing is strictly better when contexts are spatially heterogeneous. When fine-tuning a language model on six interleaved GLUE tasks, task-conditioned routing at a fixed adapter budget outperforms LoRA and static localized adapters, matching per-task adapters with 6x budgets. At decoder scale on Qwen2.5-1.5B, both routed variants outperform static baselines across eight commonsense benchmarks, with cell-wise routing achieving +3.9 points over LoRA at matched active capacity.
♻ ☆ Mingbird: A Local-First Agent Harness Enabling Small Open Models to Complete Real Tasks
Hao Wang, Ting Huang
Small open-weight language models now run on a laptop, but they fail at multi-step work in ways a large model does not: they stop with the files missing, repeat one action until the budget ends, and report success without checking. At the one-to-nine-billion-parameter tier the harness supplies almost all of the procedural control that a large model supplies for itself.We present Mingbird, an open-source harness for that tier on a consumer graphics card: 12k lines of Python, a 628-test suite, and eighteen mechanisms for context economy, completion, time budget and the host boundary, plus a crawl guard outside those four groups. Its design point is completion control: the harness runs executable checks on the working directory and refuses a claim of completion when they fail.Five evaluations accompany it. A configuration-surface audit finds four defects that the 625-test suite of v3.1.1 missed, each because a test checked a property adjacent to the one that broke. A ten-task probe suite gives each mechanism a reason to fire and reports where the change reaches the artifact, including one mechanism that cuts the signal it protects. Disabling five of six completion-time checks costs 0.152 of the artifact score on eighteen held-out tasks, three replications per cell (95% CI [+0.064, +0.246], Holm p = 0.014). On a 288-cell matrix the harness reaches 0.886 against 0.631, 0.479 and 0.405 for three open scaffolds, and on τ 2 -bench it ranks first or tied first in all three domains against the arms evaluated.Tasks, scorer, source and every per-cell result are public.
comment: 67 pages, 30 tables, 4 figures. v2: substantially revised (18 mechanisms, the 288-cell v1.5.0 matrix, five evaluations). Code, benchmark protocol, scoring code and all per-cell results: https://github.com/Mingbird/Mingbird-agent
♻ ☆ In-Context Optimization for Retrieval-Augmented Generation: A Gradient-Descent Perspective NIPS 2026
Mingchen Li, Jiatan Huang, Chuxu Zhang, Liang Zhao, Hong Yu
In-context learning has recently been linked to implicit gradient descent in linear self-attention models, suggesting that context can induce a forward-pass update. Retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) also relies on context, but retrieved documents are usually treated as static evidence rather than signals for adaptation. We study RAG as an in-context optimization process. First, we show that one linear self-attention layer can implement one gradient-descent step on a unified linearized RAG objective covering both projection-based and dot-product retrieval interfaces. This gives an exact regime where retrieval-augmented prediction and in-context optimization coincide. We use this result not as a literal model of LLM computation, but as a guide for adapting the interaction between queries and retrieved evidence. We then test the boundary of this correspondence: it remains stable under controlled linear extensions, but becomes feature-distribution dependent under nonlinear architectures. Finally, we turn this view into a lightweight method for frozen RAG LLMs. The method keeps the retriever and backbone fixed, and predicts a context-conditioned update to a generator-side evidence-use interface. Across seven QA benchmarks, two retrievers, and two frozen LLM backbones, this forward-only update improves a shared-interface baseline, transfers to held-out tasks, and approaches test-time gradient adaptation at much lower per-query cost.

♻ ☆ RICE-PO: Turning Retrieval Interactions into Credit Signals for Reasoning Agents NIPS 2026
Mingchen Li, Hansi Zeng, Zhuo Qian, Jiatan Huang, Sunjae Kwon, Hamed Zamani, Hong Yu
Retrieval is increasingly moving from one-shot matching toward interactive reasoning, where language agents iteratively inspect evidence, reformulate queries, and search again. Training such agents raises a credit-assignment challenge: executable actions such as queries or summaries can be directly evaluated by the retriever, while latent reasoning steps are not directly observable and only affect future executable actions. This asymmetry makes outcome-level reward assignment unreliable, as the same final reward may credit reasoning steps that did not actually shape retrieval success. We propose RICE-PO, a critic-free policy optimization framework that converts retrieval interactions into localized learning signals. RICE-PO selects high-uncertainty executable actions as anchors, evaluates local counterfactual branches using retrieval metrics, and propagates credit to latent reasoning steps only when reasoning-to-action influence is strong and future residual effects are stable. On BRIGHT and BEIR, RICE-PO consistently outperforms prompt-based agents and group-based RL baselines under the same retriever setting. These results show that the structure of agent-environment interaction itself can provide useful supervision for training reasoning-based retrieval agents.

♻ ☆ MamaReason: Unfinished Reasoning and Answer Recovery in Clinical Language Models
Thanni Adewuyi, Adepoju Jeremiah Moyondafoluwa, Dorcas Amapu, Dieko Whenu, Sylvia Essessien, Sandra Oko-Oba, Pelumi Onafadeji, Lily Aimas-Love, Samuel Omolaiye, AbdulQahhar Okenla, Abiodun Adereni
Answer continuation often returns a diagnosis already stated in unfinished clinical reasoning. We study this behavior in MamaReason, adapted from dense Gemma 4 31B, on MamaBench's 217 paired maternal and child-health presentations. An explicit-commitment extractor identifies a diagnosis in 75 of 94 failed RL first passes and 75 of 93 failed SFT first passes. The continuation matches that diagnosis in 67/75 RL cases and 58/75 SFT cases, using diagnosis strings alone. Reading these commitments without additional generation recovers 30 of the 40 RL pairs restored by continuation under the archived correctness labels. Recurrence controls distinguish routine mentions from repeated answer selection. An offline second-commitment trigger measures the tradeoff between earlier readout and paired accuracy. The resulting decomposition separates diagnostic content already present in a trace, completion of an accepted answer, and paired diagnostic correctness. For clinical language-model evaluation, these are distinct outcomes that become visible when the same reasoning trace is examined through multiple readouts.
♻ ☆ Asymmetric Discourse Homogenization on Reddit Was a Text-Length Artifact: A Reanalysis
Fengming Liu
In the first version of this paper I reported that conservative Reddit users began writing more alike after ChatGPT was released while progressive users did not. That result came from a similarity measure that rises and falls with comment length. I redo every analysis of the original paper on all 6,056,593 comments with a sentence encoder and a dispersion measure computed within length strata, and the conservative break goes away. The two camps show no reliable difference in how within-group similarity changed after late 2022, and the ChatGPT date behaves like any of the more than two thousand other cutoffs I test. The matched flair comparison, the cumulative AI index, the stayer panel and the entropy signature each lose their support once length is held fixed. The one change that survives is older. Left and right comments drew closer to each other between 2019 and 2022, and that convergence was complete before ChatGPT arrived. I end with what this implies for studies that measure homogenization with embeddings.
comment: 18 pages, 4 figures, 5 tables. This version replaces v1 entirely: the v1 headline finding (asymmetric conservative homogenization after ChatGPT) was an artifact of comment length in the similarity measure. The artifact is documented in general form in arXiv:2611.21519
♻ ☆ Continued Training of Continuous Language Flows with Token Identity Squared Supervision
Jiawei Jiang, Zhaoxiang Zang, Zhengtao Yu
Diffusion and flow models have recently been extended to language modeling, generating text through continuous denoising in token embedding spaces. Pretrained models provide token representations and contextual prediction capabilities, but improving generation with limited additional training requires an appropriate connection between supervision and continuous generation. When continuous predictions are formed by weighting token embeddings with predicted probabilities, different probability distributions can yield the same vector and are therefore indistinguishable under a continuous reconstruction objective based on mean squared error (MSE). We propose Token Identity Brier Flow (TIBF) to apply squared supervision directly to token identities. TIBF fixes token embeddings, combines network scores with token-matching scores under a Gaussian observation model, supervises token probabilities using the Brier loss, and uses probability-weighted embeddings for continuous generation. We show that, for fixed token embeddings, the squared error in token posterior prediction controls the squared errors of the continuous denoising prediction and the flow velocity. A risk decomposition separates uncertainty inherent in the observation from posterior fitting error, motivating continued training that preserves pretrained embeddings and focuses on probability prediction. From common pretrained initializations and under matched continuation budgets, TIBF achieves mean Gen.PPL values of 34.87 on OWT and 80.08 on LM1B, outperforming continuous-MSE and CE controls. Further evaluations show improvements across multiple sampling budgets. Code is available at https://github.com/Jiangjiawei-kust/TIBF.
comment: 49 pages, including appendices; 7 figures. Code: https://github.com/Jiangjiawei-kust/TIBF
♻ ☆ RL Post-Training Builds Compositional Reasoning Strategies NeurIPS 2026
Azwar Abdulsalam, Nishil Patel, Andrew Saxe
Does RL post-training merely amplify primitive skills already latent in a base model, or can it compose primitive skills into new higher-level strategies? We study this question in a fully observable rewrite-grammar environment where the pretraining distribution is known and every generated rewrite can be audited. A Transformer is pretrained on primitive symbol-rewrite chains and post-trained on a Trace-based reasoning task with only a binary final-answer reward. RL solves held-out problems that remain rarely solved by the pretrained model even under much larger sampling budgets, while rejection fine-tuning improves early but plateaus. Trace analysis shows that RL reorganizes primitive competence through a phased compositional mechanism: it first strengthens primitive reductions, then discovers valid composed procedures. These include sequential compositions, which collapse ordered chains of primitive contractions, and parallel compositions, which combine independent primitive contractions in a single step. The composed procedures are not isolated samples; they are reused and consolidated into a stable repertoire. Comparing RL with rejection fine-tuning shows that the key difference is not exploration volume but selectivity: RFT produces many shortcut-like rewrites, much of them invalid, whereas RL concentrates exploration into valid reusable structure. Pretraining ablations show that the emergence of compositional strategies is gated not by primitive exposure alone, but by whether pretraining organizes primitive competence into reduction procedures that RL can later compress. The base model provides weak procedural ingredients; RL builds them into reliable higher-level strategies.
comment: Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 (poster)
